on each side of (
B.96
), we conclude that this equation is equivalent to the system

(B.99)

r_{3}+\mathcal{L}f_{3}=0,\qquad r_{2}+\mathcal{L}f_{2}=2\nu(z_{2}^{2}+z_{4}^{2}),\qquad r_{1}+\mathcal{L}f_{1}=0,\qquad r_{0}-2f_{0}^{\prime}=\nu.

3. We now solve (
B.99
). We observe that the operator
\mathcal{L}
acts on
\mathcal{P}_{j}
; below we specifically focus on
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. In polar coordinates
z_{2}=\rho\cos{\theta}
and
z_{4}=\rho\sin{\theta}
, we simply have
\mathcal{L}=\partial_{\theta}
. It follows that
\mathcal{L}
is a bijection of
\mathcal{P}_{3}
(and more generally, of
\mathcal{P}_{j}
for
j
odd); therefore we can find
f_{3}
with
r_{3}+\mathcal{L}f_{3}=0
; likewise we can find
f_{1}
such that
r_{1}+\mathcal{L}f_{1}=0
.

The range of
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is however not all of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
: it consists of elements of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
which average to
0
on the orbit
\{z_{2}^{2}+z_{4}^{2}=1\}
. Therefore, if we set

(B.100)

\nu\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{4\pi}\int_{z_{2}^{2}+z_{4}^{2}=1}r_{2},

then we can find
f_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
such that
r_{2}+\mathcal{L}f_{2}=\nu(z_{2}^{2}+z_{4}^{2})
. We finally construct
f_{0}
by integrating
f_{0}^{\prime}
in the identity
r_{0}-2f_{0}^{\prime}=\nu
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark B.2
.

From the formula (
B.100
), and the relation (
B.98
) between
r
and
r_{2}
, we see that if
r
has no term in
\xi_{1}x_{2}^{2}
or
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}^{2}
, then
\nu
vanishes – hence so does the coefficient
\mu
defined in (
B.79
). This will serve when looking at the example of §
E.1
.

B.3.4.
Generating function

We complete Theorem
B.4
with a result on the existence of a generating function of
\kappa
near
0
with useful global properties. This justifies that one can quantize
\kappa
into a Fourier integral operator of the form (
B.11
). This will serve in §
D
.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\kappa
be the symplectomorphism produced by Theorem
B.4
. There exists a smooth function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i)

\phi
is quadratic outside a neighborhood of
0
;

(ii)

The map
(x,\xi)\mapsto\big(\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi),\xi\big)
is a smooth diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
;

(iii)

For
(x,\xi)
in a neighborhood of
0
,

(B.101)

\kappa\big(\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi),\xi\big)=\big(x,\partial_{x}\phi(x,\xi)\big).

Proof.

1. Since
\partial_{x}\pi\circ\kappa(0)
is invertible, there exists a smooth function
{\widetilde{\phi}}
defined on a neighborbood of
0
such that

(B.102)

\kappa\big(\partial_{\xi}{\widetilde{\phi}}(x,\xi),\xi\big)=\big(x,\partial_{x}{\widetilde{\phi}}(x,\xi)\big).

We now Taylor-expand
{\widetilde{\phi}}
near
(x,0)
with respect to
\xi
; then we expand the remainder
r
near
(\xi,0)
with respect to
x
:

(B.103)

{\widetilde{\phi}}(x,\xi)={\widetilde{\phi}}(x,0)+\xi\cdot r(x,\xi)={\widetilde{\phi}}_{1}(x)+{\widetilde{\phi}}_{2}(\xi)+\xi\cdot{\widetilde{\Phi}}(x,\xi)x,

for some smooth functions
{\widetilde{\phi}}_{1},{\widetilde{\phi}}_{2},{\widetilde{\Phi}}
defined on a neighborhood of
0
. We extend these functions into smooth functions bounded together with all their derivatives on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Also, we remark from the invertibility of
\partial_{x}\pi\circ\kappa(0)
that the matrix
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}{\widetilde{\phi}}(0)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(0)
is invertible.

2. Fix
\chi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a smooth function equal to
1
on
[-1,1]
and
0
outside
(-2,2)
. For
r\in(0,1]
, we introduce, with
z=(x,\xi)
:

(B.104)

\begin{gathered}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(z)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \chi\left(\dfrac{|z|}{r}\right){\widetilde{\Phi}}(z)+\left(1-\chi\left(\dfrac{|z|}{r}\right)\right){\widetilde{\Phi}}(0),\\
\phi(z)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \chi\left(r|z|\right){\widetilde{\phi}}_{1}(x)+\chi\left(r|z|\right){\widetilde{\phi}}_{2}(\xi)+x\cdot\Phi(z)\xi.\end{gathered}

Then
\Phi={\widetilde{\Phi}}
in the ball of radius
r
, and hence
\phi={\widetilde{\phi}}
in this ball (as long as
r
is small enough); this implies that (iii) holds. Likewise
\Phi=\Phi(0)
outside the ball of radius
2r
, therefore
\phi
is quadratic outside this ball; this implies that (i) holds.

3. We now check that
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi
never vanishes as long as
r
is sufficiently small. Taking the derivatives with respect to
(x,\xi)
of the first two terms in the definition (
B.104
) produces a term
O(r)
. For the third term, we write

(B.105)

\Phi(z)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(0)+\chi\left(\dfrac{|z|}{r}\right)z\cdot\Psi(z).

for some smooth function
\Psi
. Taking one derivative with respect to
x,\xi
produces a term
O(1)
; two derivatives produces
O(r^{-1})
. Therefore, we end up with

(B.106)

\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x,\xi)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(0)+O(r).

Picking
r
sufficiently small ensures that
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi
is invertible at every point. Moreover, outside a compact set we have
\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi)=\Phi(0)x
. It follows that the map
(x,\xi)\mapsto\big(\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi),\xi\big)
is proper. By Hadamard’s global inversion theorem, see e.g.
[
KP02
, Theorem 6.2.4]
, this map is a diffeomorphism.
∎

B.3.5.
Construction of a
SU(2)
-gauge

To complete the proof of Theorem
B.1
, it suffices to construct a suitable gauge transformation.

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

1. Let
\kappa,\lambda,\mu
be the symplectomorphism and functions constructed in Theorem
B.4
. We note that
q
vanishes on
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
while
q\circ\kappa
vanishes on a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
. It follows that
p_{j}\circ\kappa(z_{1},0)
vanishes on a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
. Therefore, for
z_{1}
sufficiently small, we have

(B.107)

\dfrac{p_{j}\circ\kappa(z)}{\lambda(z_{1})(1+\mu(z_{1})z_{3})}=\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}(2),\qquad\alpha_{j}(z_{1})=\dfrac{\nabla\big(p_{j}\circ\kappa\big)(z_{1},0)}{\lambda(z_{1})}.

In particular,

(B.108)

\dfrac{{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa(z)}{\lambda(z_{1})(1+\mu(z_{1})z_{3})}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle\sigma_{j}+\mathcal{O}(2).

2. We take the determinant on both sides of (
B.108
) and obtain, thanks to (
B.50
):

(B.109)

-\det\left[\dfrac{{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa(z)}{\lambda(z_{1})(1+\mu(z_{1})z_{3})}\right]=|{\tilde{z}}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(4)=-\det\left[\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle\sigma_{j}\right]+\mathcal{O}(3).

Identifying terms that are quadratic in
{\tilde{z}}
, we deduce that

(B.110)

|{\tilde{z}}|^{2}=-\det\left[\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle\sigma_{j}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle^{2}.

It follows from Lemma
B.2
that there exists a smooth map
U
defined on a neighborhood of
0
to
SU(2)
such that

(B.111)

U(z_{1})^{*}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\langle\alpha_{j}(z_{1}),{\tilde{z}}\right\rangle\sigma_{j}\right)U(z_{1})=z_{2}\sigma_{1}+z_{3}\sigma_{2}+z_{4}\sigma_{1}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ {\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z).

Plugging (
B.111
) into (
B.108
) yields

(B.112)

U(z_{1})^{*}{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa(z)U(z_{1})=\lambda(z_{1})\big(1+\mu(z_{1})z_{3}\big)\big({\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(2)\big).

3. Thanks to (
B.112
), to prove the theorem it suffices to work with a symbol
{\not{\partial}}{}
such that

(B.113)

q(z)=q_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(4),\qquad{\operatorname{Tr\ }}{\not{\partial}}{}(z)=0,\qquad{\not{\partial}}{}(z)={\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(2),

and construct
U:\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow SU(2)
such that
U(z)^{*}{\not{\partial}}{}(z)U(z)={\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(3)
.

Below we will look for
U(z)=e^{iH(z)}
, where
H=\sum_{k=1}^{3}h_{k+1}\sigma_{k}
,
h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. In particular,
U(z)={\operatorname{Id}}+iH(z)+\mathcal{O}(2)
, hence
U(z)^{*}{\not{\partial}}{}(z)U(z)={\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(3)
is equivalent to

(B.114)

{\not{\partial}}{}(z)\big({\operatorname{Id}}+iH(z)\big)=H(z){\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z).

Writing
{\not{\partial}}{}(z)={\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)+R(z)+\mathcal{O}(3)
where
R=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}r_{j+1}\sigma_{j}
,
r_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
Then (
B.114
) reduces to

(B.115)

R(z)=i[H(z),{\not{\partial}}{}_{0}(z)].

Using the decomposition of
R
and
H
in terms of Pauli matrices, we end up with

(B.116)

2\sum_{j=1}^{3}r_{j}\sigma_{j}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{3}h_{k+1}z_{\ell+1}\cdot i[\sigma_{k},\sigma_{\ell}]=\sum_{j,k,\ell=1}^{3}h_{k+1}z_{\ell+1}\cdot 2{\varepsilon}_{jk\ell}\sigma_{j}.

This reduces to a
3\times 3
system:

(B.117)

\left\{\begin{matrix}z_{3}h_{4}-z_{4}h_{3}=r_{2}\\
z_{4}h_{2}-z_{2}h_{4}=r_{3}\\
z_{2}h_{3}-z_{3}h_{2}=r_{4}\end{matrix}\right..

This is a cross product-type system: it takes the form
z\times h=r
with
z=[z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}]^{\top}
,
h=[h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}]^{\top}
(the unknown) and
r=[r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}]^{\top}
(the data). In particular, it is degenerate: it has a solution if and only if
z\cdot r=0
. We verify that
r
satisfies this condition: from
q(z)=q_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(4)
, we have

(B.118)

(z_{2}+r_{2})^{2}+(z_{3}+r_{3})^{2}+(z_{4}+r_{4})^{2}=z_{2}^{2}+z_{3}^{2}+z_{4}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(4).

Identifying the homogeneous terms of degree
3
on both sides, we deduce that
z\cdot r=0
, i.e.

(B.119)

z_{2}r_{2}+z_{3}r_{3}+z_{4}r_{4}=0.

Thanks to the relation
z\cdot r=0
, the last equation of (
B.117
) automatically holds when the first two do. Therefore, the system (
B.117
) reduces from
3
to
2
equations:

(B.120)

\left\{\begin{matrix}z_{3}h_{4}-z_{4}h_{3}=r_{2}\\
z_{4}h_{2}-z_{2}h_{4}=r_{3}\end{matrix}\right..

4. We now focus on solving (
B.120
). Thanks to the constraint (
B.120
), we observe that
r_{2}
has no terms in
z_{2}^{2}
. In particular, we can write it as
r_{2}=z_{3}h_{4}-z_{4}h_{3}
for some
h_{3},h_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. To solve (
B.120
), hence (
B.117
), it remains to find
h_{2}
.

Again, thanks to (
B.120
),
r_{3}
has no terms in
z_{3}^{2}
and we can write it as
r_{3}=z_{4}\ell_{2}-z_{2}\ell_{4}
with
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Plugging this and
r_{2}=z_{3}h_{4}-z_{4}h_{3}
in (
B.120
), we obtain

(B.121)

\begin{gathered}0=z_{2}(z_{3}h_{4}-z_{4}h_{3})+z_{3}(z_{4}\ell_{2}-z_{2}\ell_{4})+z_{4}r_{4}.\\
\Rightarrow\qquad 0=z_{2}z_{3}(h_{4}-\ell_{4})+z_{4}(r_{4}+z_{3}\ell_{2}-z_{2}h_{3})=0.\end{gathered}

In particular,
h_{4}-\ell_{4}=\alpha z_{4}
for some (smooth) function
\alpha
of
z_{1}
. We then have

(B.122)

r_{3}=z_{4}\ell_{2}-z_{2}\ell_{4}=z_{4}\ell_{2}-z_{2}(h_{4}-\alpha z_{4})=z_{4}(\ell_{2}-\alpha z_{2})-z_{2}h_{4}.

It suffices then to set
h_{2}=\ell_{2}-\alpha z_{2}
to solve (
B.120
). This in turns produces
H
, hence a map
U
defined on a neighborhood
\Omega
of
0
, with values in
SU(2)
, such that (
B.7
) holds.

5. It remains to extend the domain
\Omega
of
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. By considering the map
U(0)^{*}\cdot U
, we may assume that
U(0)=0
. The exponential map from the Lie algebra
su(2)
(
i
times traceless
2\times 2
Hermitian matrices) to the Lie group
SU(2)
is a diffeomorphism on a neighborhood of
0
to a neighborhood of
{\operatorname{Id}}
. Therefore, after potentially shrinking
\Omega
, there exists a smooth map
A:\Omega\rightarrow su(2)
such that
U(x,\xi)=e^{A(x,\xi)}
. It suffices then to extend
A
to an arbitrary smooth function
\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow su(2)
, and
U
accordingly by taking the exponential map. ∎

B.3.6.
Expressions of
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
in terms of
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
and
\kappa
:

Having proved Theorem
B.1
, we now compute the vector field
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
defined in (
A.24
):

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\kappa
and
\lambda
such that (
B.7
) holds. Then along
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
, we have the relations:

(B.123)

\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa=\lambda,\qquad V_{\not{\partial}}{}=d\kappa\big(\lambda(x_{1})\partial_{x_{1}}\big).

Proof.

1. Thanks to (
B.7
) and (
B.43
), we have, along
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
, the relations

(B.124)

\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa=\lambda_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{},\qquad M_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}=U^{*}\cdot M_{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa\cdot U,\qquad d\kappa(H_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{})=U^{*}\cdot H_{\not{\partial}}{}\cdot U.

We deduce that along
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
,

(B.125)

d\kappa(V_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{})=-d\kappa\left(\dfrac{{\operatorname{Tr\ }}(M_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}H_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{})}{2\lambda^{2}_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}}\right)=-\dfrac{{\operatorname{Tr\ }}(M_{\not{\partial}}{}H_{\not{\partial}}{})}{2\lambda^{2}_{\not{\partial}}{}}=V_{\not{\partial}}{}.

The conclusion follows from
\lambda_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}=\lambda
and
V_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}=\lambda(x_{1})\partial_{1}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
.
∎

B.4.
Quantization

In this section we prove Theorem
B.2
. As a preliminary, we review some standard facts about pseudodifferential operators. Given a (potentially matrix-valued) symbol
A
, the Weyl quantization of
A
is the operator

(B.126)

A^{w}u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{i\xi(x-y)/h}A\left(\dfrac{x+y}{2},\xi\right)u(y)\cdot\dfrac{dyd\xi}{(2\pi h)^{2}};

we refer to
A^{w}
as a pseudodifferential operator. We then have the following facts:

(a)

The adjoint of
A^{w}
is pseudodifferential, with symbol
A^{*}+O(h)
; moreover
A^{w}
is selfadjoint if and only if
A
is Hermitian-valued.

(b)

The composition of two pseudodifferential operators
A^{w}
and
B^{w}
is also pseudodifferential. Its symbol, commonly denoted
A\#B
, satisfies

(B.127)

A\#B=AB+\dfrac{h}{2i}\{A,B\}+O(h^{2}).

(c)

If
\det A
is bounded below, then for
h
sufficiently small the operator
A^{w}
is invertible. It inverse is a pseudodifferential operator with symbol
A^{-1}+O(h)
.

(d)

If
C={\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
, then there exists a symbol
B={\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
such that
(B^{w})^{*}B^{w}=C^{w}
.

(e)

If
U
is such that
U^{*}U={\operatorname{Id}}
, then there exists a symbol
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}=U+O(h)
and
(\mathcal{U}^{w})^{*}\mathcal{U}={\operatorname{Id}}
.

We now review a few facts about Fourier integral operators. Given a function
\phi
satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
B.6
, we define an operator
F
by

(B.128)

Fu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\frac{i}{h}(\phi(x,\xi)-y\xi)}u(y)\cdot\big|\det\partial_{x\xi}\phi(x,\xi)\big|^{1/2}\dfrac{dyd\xi}{(2\pi h)^{2}}.

We then have the three following facts:

(f)

The operator
F
is nearly unitary:
F^{*}F
is a pseudodifferential operator with symbol
C={\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
;

(g)

Egorov’s theorem: if
A
is a symbol bounded together with all its derivatives, then
F^{*}A^{w}F
is a pseudodifferential operator and its symbol is
A\circ\kappa+O(h)
.

(h)

Composing
F
with a pseudodifferential operator produces an operator of the form (
B.11
).

Standard references for these facts are
[
Z12
, §4, §11]
and
[
GS94
, §10]
(where
h=1
, but the proofs apply here without change).

Proof of Theorem
B.2
.

1. Let
\kappa,\lambda,\mu,U
be the quantities produced by Theorem
B.1
. Let
\phi
constructed according to Lemma
B.6
and let
F
be the Fourier integral operator (
B.128
). By (g),
F^{*}\not{D}F
is a pseudodifferential operator with symbol
{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa+O(h)
.

Moreover, by (f),
F^{*}F
is a pseudodifferential operator with symbol
{\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
. By (d), there exists
B={\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
such that
F^{*}F=(B^{w})^{*}B^{w}
. The operator
B
is invertible, and its inverse has symbol
{\operatorname{Id}}+O(h)
by (c). We deduce that
F_{2}=F(B^{w})^{-1}
is unitary. Therefore,
F_{2}^{*}\not{D}F_{2}
is a selfadjoint pseudodifferential operator, with symbol
{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa+O(h)
.

Because of (e), there exists a symbol
\mathcal{U}=U+O(h)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{w}
is unitary. The operator
F_{3}=\mathcal{U}^{w}F_{2}
is then unitary; hence
\mathcal{F}_{3}^{*}\not{D}\mathcal{F}_{3}
is a Hermitian pseudodifferential operator with symbol

(B.129)

U\cdot{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa\cdot U^{*}+O(h).

Expanding transversely to
(x_{1},0)
the
O(h)
-remainder above, we may write (
B.129
) as

(B.130)

\big(U\cdot{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa\cdot U^{*}\big)(x,\xi)+hR(x_{1})+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

3. We note that
R(x_{1})
is Hermitian-valued because
\mathcal{F}_{3}
is unitary and
\not{D}
,
U\cdot{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa\cdot U^{*}
are Hermitian (as operators and symbols, respectively), see (a). Therefore, we may decompose
R
as

(B.131)

R(x_{1})=s(x_{1})+\lambda(x_{1}){\widetilde{R}}(x_{1}),\qquad{\widetilde{R}}(x_{1})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}r_{j}(x_{1})\sigma_{j}.

Let
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0}=x_{2}\sigma_{1}+\xi_{2}\sigma_{2}+\xi_{1}\sigma_{3}
. We claim that there exists a scalar, never-vanishing symbol
c
such that on a neighborhood of
0
,

(B.132)

i\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0},c\}+{\widetilde{R}}c=\mathcal{O}(1).

Indeed, with the decomposition
{\widetilde{R}}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}r_{j}\sigma_{j}
, (
B.132
) reduces to three equations:

(B.133)

\left\{\begin{matrix}-i\partial_{\xi_{2}}c+r_{1}c=\mathcal{O}(1)\\
i\partial_{x_{2}}c+r_{2}c=\mathcal{O}(1)\\
i\partial_{x_{1}}c+r_{3}c=\mathcal{O}(1)\end{matrix}\right..

We then verify that the symbol
c
defined on a neighborhood of
0
by

(B.134)

c(x,\xi)=\exp\left(i\int_{0}^{x_{1}}r_{3}\right)\big(1-i\xi_{2}r_{1}(x_{1})+ix_{2}r_{2}(x_{1})\big),

and extended to a never-vanishing symbol on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, satisfies (
B.133
) hence (
B.132
).

4. Below we write
A\equiv B
to denote two symbols
A
and
B
such that

(B.135)

A-B=\mathcal{O}(3)+O(h^{2})+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)

on a neighborhood of
0
. We define the operator
\mathcal{F}=c^{w}\mathcal{F}_{3}
. By (h), this is an operator of the form (
B.11
). By Step 2,
\mathcal{F}\not{D}\mathcal{F}^{-1}
is a pseudodifferential operator. To compute its symbol (in a neighborhood of
0
), we use (
B.130
); Theorem
B.1
; the composition rule (b) and inversion rule (c); and (
B.131
). This gives:

(B.136)

\begin{gathered}c\#\big(U\cdot{\not{\partial}}{}\circ\kappa\cdot U^{*}+hR\big)\#\big(c^{-1}+O(h)\big)=c\#\big({\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}+hR+\mathcal{O}(3)\big)\#\big(c^{-1}+O(h)\big)\\
\equiv{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}+\dfrac{hc}{2i}\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{},c^{-1}\}+\dfrac{hc^{-1}}{2i}\{c,{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}\}+hR\equiv{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}+hs+hc^{-1}\left(i\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{},c\}+\lambda{\widetilde{R}}c\right).\end{gathered}

From (
B.134
), we note that
\partial_{\xi_{1}}c=0
. Since
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}=(\lambda+\mu\xi_{1}){\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0}
, we obtain

(B.137)

\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{},c\}=\lambda\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0},c\}+\mu\partial_{x_{1}}c\cdot{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0}=\lambda\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0},c\}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Plugging this in (
B.136
), and using (
B.132
) for
c
, we conclude that the symbol of
\mathcal{F}\not{D}\mathcal{F}^{-1}
is:

(B.138)

{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}+hs+hc^{-1}\lambda\left(i\{{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}_{0},c\}+{\widetilde{R}}c\right)\equiv{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}+hs.

This completes the proof of Theorem
B.2
.∎

Remark B.3
.

We comment briefly on the case
\pi(\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{})
and
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. In this case, the proof of Theorems
B.1
and
B.4
already produces
\kappa
on neighborhood of arbitrary bounded subsets of
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
; this uses essentially that vector bundles over
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
are trivial as
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
is simply connected
[
M01
, §1.3]
. We can moreover ensure the existence of a generating function
\phi
such that the conclusions of Lemma
B.6
hold on neighborhoods of
(-L,L)\times\{0\}^{3}
, for arbitrary
L>0
(instead of simply a neighborhood of
0
). Therefore, the equation (
B.14
) holds with a remainder
R
whose symbol is
\mathcal{O}(3)+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
on neighborhoods of
(-L,L)\times\{0\}^{3}
.

Chapter C
Analysis of the model operator

C.1.
Setting and main result

In this chapter we assume given two smooth functions
\lambda:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\mu:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and we assume that
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
is the operator that emerges in §
B.4
:

(C.1)

\displaystyle\not{\mathfrak{D}}=

\displaystyle\left(h\lambda(x_{1})D_{1}+\dfrac{h\lambda^{\prime}(x_{1})}{2i}+h^{2}D_{1}\mu(x_{1})D_{1}-h^{2}\dfrac{\mu^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})}{4}\right)\sigma_{3}

(C.2)

\displaystyle+\left(\lambda(x_{1})+h\mu(x_{1})D_{1}+\dfrac{h\mu^{\prime}(x_{1})}{2i}\right)\left[\begin{matrix}0&x_{2}-h\partial_{2}\\
x_{2}+h\partial_{2}&0\end{matrix}\right]+hs(x_{1}).

We perform a semiclassical analysis for initial data originally concentrated (in phase space) at the point
0\in\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
. Specifically, we show that solutions to

(C.3)

\left(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}\right)F=0,\qquad F(0,x)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot A\left(\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{h}}\right),\qquad A\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

split, up to a small remainder in
L^{2}
, as:

•

a semiclassical wavepacket propagating rightwards along
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
, at speed
\lambda(x_{1})
;

•

an orthogonal wave that disperses very rapidly.

In particular, coherent propagation only happens rightwards (here
\lambda>0
;
\lambda<0
would yield leftward propagation). Forcing propagation in the opposite direction – for instance by adequately preparing the initial data – immediately destroys the coherent state structure.

In the framework of §
B
, the operator
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
is a semiclassical operator, whose principal symbol

(C.4)

{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}(x,\xi)=\big(\lambda(x_{1})+\mu(x_{1})\xi_{1}\big)\left[\begin{matrix}\xi_{1}&x_{2}-i\xi_{2}\\
x_{2}+i\xi_{2}&-\xi_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

satisfies
\bm{(\mathcal{A}_{1}})
-
\bm{(\mathcal{A}_{2}})
from §
A.3.1
. It forms a family of
2\times 2
Hermitian traceless matrices; its eigenvalues are repeated along
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}^{-1}(0)
, which contains
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
. Moreover, along
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
the vector field
V_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}
and scalar
\lambda_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}
from §
A.3.1
are given by:

(C.5)

V_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}(x_{1},0)=\lambda(x_{1})\partial_{1},\qquad\lambda_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}(x_{1},0)=\lambda(x_{1}).

To state quantitatively our main result, we first let
x_{t}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}

be the solution of the ODE

(C.6)

\dot{x^{+}_{t}}=\lambda(x_{t}^{+}),\qquad x_{0}^{+}=0.

In particular,
(x_{t}^{+},0)
is the integral curve of
V_{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}
starting at
0
.
We then define

(C.7)

\rho_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{\lambda(x_{t})}{\lambda(0)};\qquad\nu_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\mu(y_{\tau})}{\rho_{\tau}^{2}}d\tau;\qquad S_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{0}^{t}s(x_{\tau}^{+})d\tau.

We finally set:

(C.8)

a_{0}(x_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2}}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]dx_{2},\qquad a_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{|4\pi\nu_{t}|^{1/2}}\cdot e^{i\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{4\nu_{t}}}\star a_{0},

where
\star
denotes convolution with respect to the
x_{1}
-variable; and the Gaussian in the definition of
a_{t}
is understood as a Dirac mass at
0
when
\nu_{t}=0
(in particular at
t=0
).

Theorem C.1
.

There exists
T>0
such that the solution
F
of (
C.3
) satisfies uniformly, for
(t,x)\in(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
h\in(0,1]
:

(C.9)

\begin{gathered}F(t,x)=\dfrac{e^{iS_{t}}}{\sqrt{\rho_{t}h}}\cdot a_{t}\left(\dfrac{x_{1}-x_{t}^{+}}{\rho_{t}\sqrt{h}}\right)e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2h}}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]+O_{L^{\infty}}\big(h^{-1/4}\big)+O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/4}\big).\end{gathered}

The expansion (
C.9
) means that the dominant part of
F
is a wavepacket propagating along
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
at physical speed
\lambda(x_{1})
plus a term in
L^{\infty}
. While the latter generally has a significant
L^{2}
mass, its amplitude is much smaller than that of the wavepacket. Visually, it simply collapses and hence loses its initial coherence.

Remark C.1
.

The coefficient
\rho_{t}
is uniformly bounded from above and below; hence the dilation by
\rho_{t}
in (
C.9
) is uniformly controlled. On the other hand,
\nu_{t}
may grow linearly with
t
, see (
C.7
). The implies that the envelop (
C.8
) may decay to
0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
(for instance, for magnetic fields
[
BBD22
, §6]
or viscous models
[
B19a
,
SD+19
]
). This is a much slower dispersion than that associated with the
O_{L^{\infty}}\big(h^{-1/4}\big)
remainder, which takes place as soon as time is turned on.

There are two cases where we can remove the limitation on
t
. The first scenario does not require additional assumptions on
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
, but it demands a special initial data:

Corollary C.1
.

If
a(x)=a_{0}(x_{1})e^{-x_{2}^{2}/2}[1,0]^{\top}
then (
C.9
) holds for any fixed
T
, without the
O_{L^{\infty}}\big(t^{-1/2}\big)
-remainder.

In particular, Corollary
C.1
predicts the existence of a wavepacket that propagates along
(x_{t}^{+},0)
. The second scenario assumes that
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
is constant:

Corollary C.2
.

If
\lambda
is constant, then (
C.9
) holds for any fixed
t
.

To prove Theorem
C.1
, we will perform an orthogonal decomposition of the initial data into Gaussian and higher-order Hermite modes in the variable
x_{2}
, see §
C.2
. A coherent state expansion quickly produces the leading order term in (
C.10
), see Theorem
C.2
. Combining this result with Theorem
B.2
allows us to recover the wavepacket constructions of
[
BB+21
,
BBD22
,
HXZ22
]
; see §
E
for applications.

Higher-order Hermite modes are more challenging to analyze. We derive an oscillatory integral formula for their time-dynamics, with a more adequate semiclassical parameter roughly equal to
\sqrt{h}
. The oscillatory phase solves the eikonal equation

(C.10)

\partial_{t}\varphi+p(x_{1},\partial_{1}\varphi)=0,\qquad p(x_{1},\xi_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \lambda(x_{1})\sqrt{1+(\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi)^{2}}.

It admits short-time solutions, which extend to longer times if the bicharacteristic flow of the Hamiltonian vector field of
p
is reversible; that is, if the map

(C.11)

x_{1}\mapsto\pi\circ e^{tH_{p}}(x_{1},\xi_{1}),\qquad\pi(x_{1},\xi_{1})=x_{1}

is invertible for all
(t,\xi_{1})
. Superposing these solutions produce a Lax-type parametrix. Its analysis through stationary and non-stationary bounds in oscillatory integrals eventually yields the
t^{-1/2}
-decay of (
C.10
).

C.2.
Reduction to
2\times 2
systems on
\mathbb{R}
and time-reversal considerations

C.2.1.
Semiclassical Hermite functions

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}=x_{2}-\partial_{2}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}=x_{2}+\partial_{2}
be the (semiclassical) creation and anhilation operators, and introduce the semiclassical Hermite functions

(C.12)

g_{0}(x_{2})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{\pi^{1/4}}e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2}};\qquad g_{n}(x_{2})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{({\mathfrak{a}}^{*})^{n}}{2^{n/2}\sqrt{n!}}g_{0}(x_{2}).

They satisfy the relations
{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}g_{n-1}=\sqrt{2n}g_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}g_{n}=\sqrt{2n}g_{n-1}
, where by convention
g_{-1}=0
. For
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, we define

(C.13)

G_{n}(x_{2})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \left[\begin{matrix}g_{n}(x_{2})\\
g_{n-1}(x_{2})\end{matrix}\right],\qquad(f\otimes G_{n})(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \left[\begin{matrix}f_{1}(x_{1})g_{n}(x_{2})\\
f_{2}(x_{1})g_{n-1}(x_{2})\end{matrix}\right].

We then set

(C.14)

g_{n,h}(x_{2})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{h^{1/4}}g_{n}\left(\dfrac{x_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right),\qquad G_{n,h}(x_{2})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{h^{1/4}}G_{n}\left(\dfrac{x_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)

C.2.2.
Block-diagonal decomposition

The relations between the Hermite functions and the creations/anhilation operators allow us to write
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
in the block-diagonal form

(C.15)

\begin{gathered}\not{\mathfrak{D}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}\right)=Lf_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{2nh}\big(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,h_{n}}f_{n}\big)\otimes G_{n,h},\qquad h_{n}=\sqrt{\dfrac{h}{2n}}\end{gathered}

where the blocks
L
,
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
are differential operators in the variable
x_{1}
only:

(C.16)

\displaystyle L\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \

\displaystyle h\lambda(x_{1})D_{1}+\dfrac{h\lambda^{\prime}(x_{1})}{2i}+h^{2}D_{1}\mu(x_{1})D_{1}-h^{2}\dfrac{\mu^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})}{4}+hs(x_{1}),

(C.17)

\displaystyle\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \

\displaystyle\left(\lambda(x_{1})\epsilon D_{1}+\dfrac{\epsilon\lambda^{\prime}(x_{1})}{2i}+2n\epsilon^{3}D_{1}\mu(x_{1})D_{1}-\dfrac{n\epsilon^{3}\mu^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})}{2}\right)\sigma_{3}

(C.18)

\displaystyle+\left(\lambda(x_{1})+2n\epsilon^{2}\mu(x_{1})D_{1}+\dfrac{n\epsilon^{2}\mu^{\prime}(x_{1})}{i}\right)\sigma_{1}+\epsilon s(x_{1}).

C.2.3.
Time-reversal considerations

The decomposition (
C.15
) reduces the system
(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})F=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to decoupled one-dimensional systems on
\mathbb{R}
, of the two following types:

(C.19)

\begin{gathered}(hD_{t}+L)f=0;\end{gathered}

(C.20)

\begin{gathered}(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon})f=0.\end{gathered}

A system with two internal degrees of freedom is time-reversal invariant if there exits an antiunitary operator
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{T}^{2}=-{\operatorname{Id}}
, and
\mathcal{T}
anticommutes with the Hamiltonian
[
GP12
,
ASV13
]
. In particular, (
C.20
) is time-reversal invariant: the antiunitary operator
\mathcal{T}
defined by

(C.21)

\mathcal{T}\psi=\left[\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right]{\overline{\psi}}=i\sigma_{2}{\overline{\psi}}.

satisfies
\mathcal{T}^{2}=-{\operatorname{Id}}
and
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\mathcal{T}=-\mathcal{T}\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
. This implies that
\psi
and
\mathcal{T}\psi
propagate in different directions of time.

Remark C.2
.

The operator
\mathcal{T}
maps a wavepacket
\psi
with orientation
u
to one with complex-orthogonal orientation
{\overline{u}}^{\perp}
. If
\psi
is concentrated at
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\notin{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}^{-1}(0)
and
u
is a positive-energy eigenvector of
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}(x_{0},\xi_{0})
, then
{\overline{u}}^{\perp}
is a negative-energy eigenvector of
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}(x_{0},\xi_{0})
. From
[
T75
]
, the dynamics of
\psi
and
\mathcal{T}\psi
respectively follow bicharacteristics of the positive and negative eigenvalue of
{\not{\mathfrak{d}}}{}
, hence propagate in opposite directions of time.

On the other hand, the bulk-edge correspondence suggests that (
C.3
) is not time reversal-invariant. Since it is a direct sum of systems of the form (
C.19
)-(
C.20
), we deduce that (
C.19
) is not time-reversal invariant. Therefore, all the information about asymmetric/topological transport for
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
comes from (
C.19
). This justifies that the wavepackets constructed in
[
BB+21
,
BBD22
,
HXZ22
]
are the dynamical analogues to topological edge states in curved settings.

There are moreover analytic differences between (
C.19
) and (
C.20
). Specifically, (
C.19
) comes with a family of traveling waves, corresponding to the dominant term in (
C.9
); and (
C.20
) disperses rapidly and generates the
L^{\infty}
-remainder in (
C.9
). We investigate these two effects in §
C.3
and §
C.4
-
C.5
.

C.3.
Propagating wavepackets

In this section, we construct an infinite-dimensional family of solutions to
(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})F=0
that at leading order travel along
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
, at speed
\lambda
. We realize the corresponding initial data in the form of wavepackets with profile transverse to
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
given by
G_{0,h}
:

(C.22)

(hD_{t}+\not{D})F=0,\qquad F(0,x)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot a_{0}\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2h}}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right],\qquad a_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).

We recall that the quantities
x_{t}^{+},\rho_{t},\nu_{t},S_{t},a_{t}
are given by (
C.6
)–(
C.8
).

Theorem C.2
.

For
a_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and
t
in compact sets, the solution to (
C.22
) satisfies:

(C.23)

\begin{gathered}F(t,x)=\dfrac{e^{iS_{t}}}{\sqrt{\rho_{t}h}}\cdot a_{t}\left(\dfrac{x_{1}-x_{t}^{+}}{\rho_{t}\sqrt{h}}\right)e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2h}}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]+O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/2}\big).\end{gathered}

Proof.

1. We observe that the initial data in (
C.22
) has profile transverse to
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
given by
G_{0,h}
. According to §
C.2
, the solution
F
to (
C.22
) takes the form

(C.24)

F(t,x)=f(t,x_{1})G_{0,h}(x_{2}),

where
f
solves
(hD_{t}+L)f=0
. We will look for
f
in the form

(C.25)

f(t,x_{1})=\dfrac{1}{h^{1/4}}a\left(t,\dfrac{x_{1}-x_{t}^{+}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \mathbb{W}[a](t,x_{1}),\qquad a(0,\cdot)=a_{0}.

2. We note that

(C.26)

\displaystyle(hD_{t}+L)\mathbb{W}[a]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}W\big[\big(\lambda(x_{t}^{+})-\dot{x_{t}^{+}}\big)D_{1}a\big]

(C.27)

\displaystyle+h\mathbb{W}\left[\left(D_{t}+\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})x_{1}D_{1}+\dfrac{\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})}{2i}+\mu(x_{t}^{+})D_{1}^{2}+s(x_{t}^{+})\right)a\right]+O_{L^{2}}(h^{3/2}).

With
\dot{x_{t}^{+}}=\lambda(x_{t}^{+})
the first term vanishes. When the second term also vanishes, we end up with

(C.28)

(hD_{t}+L)\mathbb{W}[a]=O_{L^{2}}(h^{3/2})

A standard Duhamel argument – see e.g.
[
BB+21
, Lemma 3.5]
– implies that
f=\mathbb{W}[a]+O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/2})
for bounded times, hence

(C.29)

F(t,x)=\mathbb{W}[a](x_{1})G_{0,h}(x_{2})+O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/2}).

Therefore, it remains to solve the equation

(C.30)

\left(D_{t}+\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})x_{1}D_{1}+\dfrac{\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})}{2i}+\mu(x_{t}^{+})D_{1}^{2}+s(x_{t}^{+})\right)a=0,\qquad a(0,\cdot)=a_{0}.

3. The (spatial) Fourier transform of the differential operator in (
C.30
) is

(C.31)

D_{t}-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})\dfrac{\xi_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}\xi_{1}}{2}+\mu(x_{t}^{+})\xi_{1}^{2}+s(x_{t}^{+}).

The operator
D_{1}\xi_{1}+\xi_{1}D_{1}
generates the semigroup of dilations
U_{r}f(\xi_{1})=e^{r/2}f(e^{r}\xi_{1})
. Therefore, if
r_{t}
is defined by
\dot{r_{t}}=\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})
with
r_{0}=0
, then

(C.32)

U_{r_{t}}^{-1}\left(D_{t}-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})\dfrac{\xi_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}\xi_{1}}{2}\right)U_{r_{t}}=\big(\dot{r_{t}}-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})\big)\dfrac{\xi_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}\xi_{1}}{2}+D_{t}=D_{t}.

We moreover note that
U_{r}^{-1}\xi_{1}^{2}U_{r}=e^{-2r}\xi_{1}^{2}
, and that
e^{r_{t}}=\rho_{t}
. We therefore obtain the relation

(C.33)

U_{r_{t}}^{-1}\left(D_{t}-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t}^{+})\dfrac{\xi_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}\xi_{1}}{2}+\mu(x_{t}^{+})\xi_{1}^{2}+s(x_{t}^{+})\right)U_{r_{t}}=D_{t}+\dfrac{\mu(x_{t}^{+})}{\rho_{t}^{2}}\xi_{1}^{2}+s(x_{t}^{+}).

We deduce from (
C.33
) that the Fourier transform of the solution to (
C.30
) is

(C.34)

\sqrt{\rho_{t}}e^{iS_{t}-i\nu_{t}\rho_{t}^{2}\xi_{1}^{2}}\widehat{a_{0}}\left(\rho_{t}\xi_{1}\right),\qquad\nu_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\mu(x_{\tau})}{\rho_{\tau}^{2}}d\tau,\qquad S_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{0}^{t}s(x_{\tau}^{+})d\tau.

The Fourier transform of (
C.34
) produces the solution to (
C.30
):

(C.35)

a(t,x_{1})=\dfrac{e^{iS_{t}}}{\sqrt{\rho_{t}}}\left(\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{|4\pi\nu_{t}|^{1/2}}e^{i\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{4\nu_{t}}}\star a_{0}\right)\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\rho_{t}}\right).

If
\nu_{t}=0
the above formula must be interpreted as a limit as
\nu_{t}\rightarrow 0
, i.e. the Gaussian in (
C.35
) is replaced by a Dirac mass. This completes the proof thanks to (
C.29
). ∎

Remark C.3
.

For the purpose of regularizing topological invariants in the bulk,
[
B19a
]
considered a Dirac operator with viscosity:

(C.36)

\not{\mathfrak{D}}=\left[\begin{matrix}D_{1}+\mu D_{1}^{2}&x_{2}-\partial_{2}\\
x_{2}+\partial_{2}&-D_{1}-\mu D_{1}^{2}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad\mu\neq 0.

Viscosity also originates naturally in topological models of active fluids and plasmas, see
[
SD+19
]
. The traveling mode for (
C.36
) produced by Theorem
C.2
is actually exact:

(C.37)

F(t,x)=\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{|4\pi\mu t|^{1/2}}\cdot e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2}}\left(e^{i\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{4\mu t}}\star a_{0}\right)(x_{1}-t)\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right].

Young’s convolution inequality reveals then that
F(t,x)=O_{L^{\infty}}(t^{-1/2}e^{-x_{2}^{2}/2})
: the edge state slowly disperses (along the interface). Hence, while added viscosity does not affect the coarse topological properties of the system, it does affect the long-time dynamics.

C.4.
WKB solutions

In §
C.3
, we constructed wavepacket solutions to
(hD_{t}+\not{D})F=0
, initially concentrated (in phase-space) at
(0,0)
and propagating rightward along
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}^{3}
, with speed
\lambda(x_{1})
. Up to semiclassical rescaling, the
x_{2}
-profile is a Gaussian, while the
x_{1}
-profile is given by (
C.35
).

We focus now on the second type of equation that arose in §
C.2.2
:

(C.38)

\left(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\right)f=0,

where
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
is the Dirac operator (
C.17
):

(C.39)

\displaystyle\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\

\displaystyle\left(\lambda(x)\epsilon D_{x}+\dfrac{\epsilon\lambda^{\prime}(x)}{2i}+2n\epsilon^{3}D_{x}\mu(x)D_{x}-\dfrac{n\epsilon^{3}\mu^{\prime\prime}(x)}{2}\right)\sigma_{3}

(C.40)

\displaystyle+\left(\lambda(x)+2n\epsilon^{2}\mu(x)D_{x}+\dfrac{n\epsilon^{2}\mu^{\prime}(x)}{i}\right)\sigma_{1}.

In (
C.40
), we intentionally dropped the subscript in
x_{1},D_{1}
and wrote
x,D_{x}
instead; we will use this convention for all of §
C.4
and most of §
C.5
.

We construct here WKB waves for (
C.38
), i.e. solutions modulo
O(\epsilon^{2})
of the form
be^{i\varphi/\epsilon}
, depending on
(t,x)\in(0,T)\in\mathbb{R}
as well as on a frequency parameter
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. The time
T
essentially quantifies how long the bicharacteristic flow associated to (
C.40
) is reversible for. To state our result, we introduce

(C.41)

\Omega_{0}=[0,T)\times\mathbb{R},\qquad\Omega=\Omega_{0}\times\mathbb{R}=[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

and the concept of admissible function:

Definition C.1
.

A function
q\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
depending implicitly on
\epsilon>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
is admissible of order
m
if there exists
m
such that for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N},\epsilon\in(0,1]
:

(C.42)

\sup_{(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega}\left\langle\xi^{2},n\right\rangle^{-m-|\alpha|}\big|\partial^{\alpha}q(t,x,\xi)\big|<\infty.

If (
C.42
) holds for all
m
, we say that
q
has order
-\infty
.

Theorem C.3
.

There exist
T>0
and
b,r,\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\Omega)
, such that:

•

\varphi
is real-valued and solves the eikonal equation

(C.43)

\partial_{t}\varphi+\lambda(x)\sqrt{1+(\partial_{x}\varphi)^{2}}=0,\qquad\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi.

•

b,r
are
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-valued and admissible of respective orders
1
and
4
, with
|b(0,x,\xi)|=1
for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
; and

(C.44)

\left(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\right)be^{i\varphi/\epsilon}=\epsilon^{2}re^{i\varphi/\epsilon}.

The function
\varphi
will satisfy some important stationary and non-stationary estimates, see Theorem
C.4
below. In §
C.5
, we will show that superposing WKB waves produces a parametrix for (
C.38
). Various non-stationary estimates on
\varphi
, proved in §
C.4.3
, allow us to derive various dispersive bounds on the parametrix. In §
C.4.5
, we use the time-reversal invariance of
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
(see §
C.2.3
) to construct a WKB wave that travels in the opposite direction.

C.4.1.
Eikonal and transport equations

Theorem
C.3
consists of constructing solutions to
(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon})f=O(\epsilon^{2})
in WKB form:

(C.45)

f(t,x,\xi)=\exp\left(i\frac{{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)}{\epsilon}\right)b(t,x,\xi),\qquad b=b_{0}+\epsilon b_{1},

where
\varphi,b_{0},b_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
are independent of
\epsilon
and satisfy the initial conditions

(C.46)

{\varphi}(0,x,\xi)=x\xi,\qquad\big|b_{0}(0,x,\xi)\big|=1,\qquad b_{1}(0,x,\xi)=0.

We now derive equations for
\varphi
,
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
. Given
\varphi
, we have the operator-valued expansion

(C.47)

\begin{gathered}\exp\left(-i\frac{{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)}{\epsilon}\right)\left(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\right)\exp\left(i\frac{{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)}{\epsilon}\right)=L_{0}+\epsilon L_{1}+\epsilon^{2}L_{2},\end{gathered}

where the differential operators
L_{0},L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are given by:

(C.48)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\varphi+\lambda(x)\big(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi\big),

(C.49)

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=D_{t}+\left(\lambda(x)D_{x}+\dfrac{\lambda^{\prime}(x)}{2i}\right)\sigma_{1}+2n\mu(x)\partial_{x}\varphi\big(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi\big)+s(x_{1}),

(C.50)

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=2n\big(\partial_{x}\varphi\mu(x)D_{x}+D_{x}\partial_{x}\varphi\mu(x)+\epsilon D_{x}\mu(x)D_{x}\big)\sigma_{3}+n\big(2\mu(x)D_{x}-i\epsilon^{2}\mu^{\prime}(x)\big)\sigma_{1}.

Hence, the equation
(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon})f=O(\epsilon^{2})
has a solution of the form (
C.45
) if
L_{0}b_{0}=0
and
L_{1}b_{0}+L_{0}b_{1}=0
. For the leading equation
L_{0}b_{0}=0
to have a solution
b_{0}\neq 0
,
L_{0}
must have a non-trivial kernel:
\det L_{0}=0
. This produces the eikonal equation:

(C.51)

(\partial_{t}\varphi)^{2}=\lambda(x)^{2}\big((\partial_{x}\varphi)^{2}+1\big),\qquad\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi.

Theorem
C.3
focuses on one branch of solutions to (
C.51
):

(C.52)

\partial_{t}\varphi+\lambda(x)\sqrt{(\partial_{x}\varphi)^{2}+1},\qquad\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi.

This is a Hamilton–Jacobi equation. We refer to
[
Z12
, §10.2]
for the standard approach to construct WKB solutions. It consists first in studying the bicharacteristic flow of the underlying symbol,
p(x,\xi)=\lambda(x)\sqrt{1+\xi^{2}}
: the flow
x_{0}\mapsto x_{t}
generated by the ODE

(C.53)

\left\{\begin{matrix}\dot{x_{t}}=\lambda(x_{t})\dfrac{\xi_{t}}{\sqrt{1+\xi_{t}^{2}}}\\
\dot{\xi_{t}}=-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t})\sqrt{1+\xi_{t}^{2}}\end{matrix}\right..

In §
C.4.2
, we prove that this flow is reversible for short time independent of the initial data
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This produces solutions to (
C.52
) expressed in terms of the reversed bicharacteristic flow. We will use this semi-explicit construction to prove non-stationary estimates for
\varphi
.

With
\varphi
solution of (
C.52
), the nullspace of
L_{0}
is not empty, and one can solve the equation
L_{0}b_{0}=0
. The solution
b_{0}
takes the form
b_{0}=\alpha u
, where
u
is a normalized vector in the nullspace of
L_{0}
, and
\alpha
is a scalar-valued function with
\alpha(0,x,\xi)=1
. For the subleading equation
L_{0}b_{1}+L_{1}b_{0}=0
to have a solution
b_{1}
, the term
L_{1}b_{0}
must be orthogonal to the kernel of
L_{0}
. This gives the equation
L_{1}(\alpha u_{0})\perp\ker L_{0}
for
\alpha
, which we solve in §
C.4.4

C.4.2.
Classical flow

We study here the flow

(C.54)

\left\{\begin{matrix}\dot{x_{t}}=\lambda(x_{t})\dfrac{\xi_{t}}{\sqrt{1+\xi_{t}^{2}}}\\
\dot{\xi_{t}}=-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t})\sqrt{1+\xi_{t}^{2}}\end{matrix}\right.,\qquad(x_{0},\xi_{0})=(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2};

see e.g.
[
GS94
, §5]
for its relation with the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
C.52
). We will use the notation:

(C.55)

F(t,x,\xi)=x_{t},\qquad G(t,x,\xi)=\xi_{t},\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

We prove uniform estimates on
F
and
G
for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t
in compact sets. While
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is not compact, the flow (
C.54
) (which is nearly homogeneous for large
\xi
) admits a natural compactification in
\xi
: with
\zeta_{t}=\arctan\xi_{t}
,
(x_{t},\zeta_{t})
solves the ODE

(C.56)

\left\{\begin{matrix}\dot{x_{t}}=\lambda(x_{t})\sin\zeta_{t}\\
\dot{\zeta}_{t}=-\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t})\cos\zeta_{t}\end{matrix}\right.,\qquad(x_{0},\zeta_{0})=(x,\zeta).

Instead of looking at (
C.56
) for initial data
(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
, we will consider initial data
(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Formally speaking, (
C.56
) extends (
C.54
) beyond
\xi=\pm\infty
.

Figure C.2.
Flow lines of (
C.56
). The dark blue region corresponds to the physical space – i.e. where (
C.54
) gets mapped to. The blue lines correspond to
\xi=\pm\infty
and separate the dark blue region from the unphysical light blue region. The flow does not connect the two regions.

We note that if
x_{t},\zeta_{t}
solve (
C.56
), then
\dot{x_{t}}
and
\dot{\zeta_{t}}
are uniformly bounded; from Cauchy–Lipschitz theory, they are defined for all times. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

(C.57)

{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)=x_{t},\quad{\widetilde{G}}(t,x,\zeta)=\zeta_{t},\quad(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
(x_{t},\zeta_{t})
solves (
C.56
) with initial data
(x_{0},\zeta_{0})=(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. While the ODE (
C.56
) is technically simpler than (
C.54
), there is a simple relation relating the two flows:

(C.58)

F(t,x,\xi)={\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\arctan\xi),\qquad G(t,x,\xi)=\tan\big({\widetilde{G}}(t,x,\arctan\xi)\big),\qquad(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Later, we will need to consider the flow of (
C.54
) as
\xi
approaches
\pm\infty
, which corresponds to
\zeta\rightarrow\pm\pi/2
. Since
\pi/2
is a regular point of
{\widetilde{F}}
, the following limits exist:

(C.59)

F_{\pm}(t,x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \lim_{\xi\rightarrow\pm\infty}F(t,x,\xi)={\widetilde{F}}\big(t,x,\pm\pi/2\big),\qquad x_{t}^{\pm}=F_{\pm}(t,0).

From (
C.56
), the functions
F_{\pm}
solve the autonomous first order ODE
\partial_{t}F_{\pm}=\pm\lambda\circ F_{\pm}
; in particular, they admit an representation in terms of a antiderivative of
1/\lambda
:

(C.60)

F_{\pm}(t,x)=\Lambda^{-1}\big(\Lambda(x)\pm t\big),\qquad x_{t}^{\pm}=\Lambda(\pm t),\qquad\Lambda(y)=\int_{0}^{y}\dfrac{dy}{\lambda(y)}.

Lemma C.1
.

All positive-order derivatives of
{\widetilde{F}},{\widetilde{G}}
are uniformly bounded for
t
in compact sets and
(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

1. We write (
C.56
) in the form

(C.61)

\dot{z_{t}}=\Phi(z_{t}),\qquad z_{t}=(x_{t},\zeta_{t}),\qquad\Phi(x,\zeta)=\left[\begin{matrix}\lambda(x)\sin\zeta\\
-\lambda^{\prime}(x)\cos\zeta\end{matrix}\right].

We note that
\Phi
is bounded together with all its derivatives. We now take
\alpha
partial derivatives with respect to
(t,z)
of (
C.61
). A recursion yields

(C.62)

\partial^{\alpha}\dot{z_{t}}=p_{\alpha}+\nabla\Phi(z_{t})\cdot\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}

where
p_{\alpha}
denotes a polynomial in the quantities
\partial^{\beta}\Phi(z_{t})
and
\partial^{\gamma}z_{t}
for
|\beta|\leq|\alpha|
and
|\gamma|\leq|\alpha|-1
. Note that
p_{\alpha}=0
when
|\alpha|=1
.

2. We now show the lemma by strong recursion on
n=|\alpha|
. For
n=|\alpha|=1
, we use (
C.62
),
\nabla\Phi
uniformly bounded, and
p_{0}=0
to obtain

(C.63)

\big|\partial^{\alpha}\dot{z_{t}}\big|\leq C|\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}|,\qquad\big|\partial^{\alpha}z_{0}\big|=1.

Set
r_{t}=|\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}|^{2}
. From (
C.63
) we deduce that
\dot{r_{t}}\leq Cr_{t}
,
r_{0}=1
. Gronwall’s lemma yields
r_{t}\leq e^{Ct}
. Therefore
\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}
is uniformly bounded for
t
in compact sets and
(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We now assume that the property holds for all
\alpha
of length smaller than some
n\geq 1
and we prove it when
|\alpha|=n+1
. According to the recursion and
f
bounded together with all its derivatives, the term
p_{\alpha}
in (
C.62
) is uniformly bounded. We deduce that

(C.64)

\big|\partial^{\alpha}\dot{z_{t}}\big|\leq C+C|\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}|,\qquad\partial^{\alpha}z_{0}=0.

Again setting
r_{t}=|\partial^{\alpha}z_{t}|^{2}
we obtain
\dot{r_{t}}\leq C+Cr_{t}
with
r_{0}=0
. An application of Gronwall’s lemma gives
r_{t}\leq e^{Ct}
. This completes the recursion. ∎

Lemma C.2
.

For every
(t,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the map
x\mapsto{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)
has range
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, there exists
T\in(0,1)
such that for all
(t,x,\zeta)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\big|\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)\big|\geq 1/2
.

Proof.

The range of
{\widetilde{F}}(t,\cdot,\zeta)
is an interval; therefore we have first to show that its supremum and infimum are
\pm\infty
. From (
C.56
),

(C.65)

\big|{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)-{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)\big|\leq|\lambda|_{\infty}t.

Taking infimum and supremum over
x\in\mathbb{R}
implies that
{\widetilde{F}}(t,\cdot,\zeta)
has range
\mathbb{R}
. Thanks to Lemma
C.1
,
\partial_{t}\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}\in C^{\infty}_{b}((0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover,
\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\xi)=\partial_{x}x=1
. Therefore,

(C.66)

\big|\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)-1\big|\leq t\cdot\sup_{(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|\partial_{t}\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)\big|\leq Ct.

It suffices to restrict
t
to sufficiently small values to deduce the lower bound on
\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}
.
∎

We recall that
\Omega_{0}=(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
and
\Omega=(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Lemma
C.2
implies that for any
(t,\zeta)\in\Omega_{0}
, the map
{\widetilde{F}}(t,\cdot,\zeta)
is a smooth diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}
. Its inverse
{\widetilde{H}}(t,\cdot,\zeta)
satisfies the relations:

(C.67)

\partial_{t}{\widetilde{H}}=-\dfrac{\partial_{t}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\xi)}{\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\xi)},\qquad\partial_{x}{\widetilde{H}}=-\dfrac{1}{\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\xi)},\qquad\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}=-\dfrac{\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\xi)}{\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\xi)}.

Thanks to Lemmas
C.1
and
C.2
, all derivatives of
{\widetilde{H}}
are in
C^{\infty}_{b}(\Omega)
.

We now set
H(t,x,\xi)={\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\arctan\xi)
, which satisfies
F(t,H(t,x,\xi),\xi)=x
. This implies that for
(t,\xi)\in(-T,T)\times\mathbb{R}
,
F(t,\cdot,\xi)
is a smooth diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}
, with inverse
H(t,\cdot,\xi)
. We stress that since all derivatives of
{\widetilde{H}}
are bounded on
\Omega
, so are all derivatives of
H
.

C.4.3.
Eikonal equation

We recall that
\Omega=[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and that
x_{t}^{\pm}
are given by (
C.60
), or equivalently (
C.59
). We define

(C.68)

I_{t}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \big(x_{t}^{-},x_{t}^{+}\big).

Theorem C.4
.

After potentially shrinking
T
:

(i)

The equation

(C.69)

\partial_{t}\varphi+p(x,\partial_{x}\varphi)=0,\qquad\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi

admits a solution
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

(ii)

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2},m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
with

(C.70)

\left|\partial_{x,t}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{m}\big(\varphi(t,x,\xi)-x\xi\big)\right|\leq C\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{1-m},\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega.

(iii)

There exists
c>0
such that:

(C.71)

\begin{gathered}\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)\big|\geq c\big(t\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2}+{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\big),\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega,\qquad x\notin I_{t};\\
\partial_{\xi}^{2}{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)\leq-c\cdot t\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3},\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega.\end{gathered}

The geometric approach to solving (
C.69
) consists of showing that the manifold

(C.72)

\big\{(t,x_{t},\tau,\xi_{t}):\ (x_{t},\xi_{t})=e^{tH_{p}}(x,\xi),\ \tau+p(x_{t},\xi_{t})=0,\ (t,x,\xi)\in\Omega\big\}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2},

which is Lagrangian, projects diffeomorphically to
\Omega_{0}\times\{0\}^{2}
; see e.g.
[
GS94
, §5]
. This holds in our case because
F(t,\cdot,\xi)
is invertible. The Lagrangian manifold (
C.72
) admits then a smooth generating function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
; that is, there exists
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that for
(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega
:

(C.73)

(x_{t},\xi_{t})=e^{tH_{p}}(x,\xi)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\partial_{t}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)+p(x_{t},\xi_{t})=0,\quad\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)=\xi_{t}.

In particular,
\partial_{x}\varphi(0,x,\xi)=\xi
, therefore

(C.74)

\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi+\varphi(0,0,\xi).

We can assume without loss of generalities that
\varphi(0,0,\xi)=0
, and therefore
\varphi
is a solution to (
C.69
). It admits a representation in terms of
(x_{t},\xi_{t})
: by computing the time-derivative of
\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)
then integrating,

(C.75)

\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)=x\xi-p(x,\xi)t+\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{\tau}\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{\tau},\xi_{\tau})d\tau=x\xi+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\xi_{\tau}\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{\tau},\xi_{\tau})-p(x_{\tau},\xi_{\tau})\right)d\tau.

In the second equality we used that
p(x,\xi)=p(x_{\tau},\xi_{\tau})
for any
\tau
. A direct computation shows that
\xi\partial_{\xi}p(x,\xi)-p(x,\xi)=-\lambda(x)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-1}=-\lambda(x)^{2}p(x,\xi)^{-1}
. Therefore, (
C.75
) simplifies to

(C.76)

\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)=x\xi-\dfrac{1}{p(x,\xi)}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(x_{\tau})^{2}ds=x\xi-\dfrac{1}{\lambda(x)\sqrt{1+\xi^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2}\circ F(\tau,x,\xi)d\tau.

Using that
H(t,x_{t},\xi)=x
, we obtain a semi-explicit formula for
\varphi
on
\Omega
, proving in particular part (i) of Theorem
C.4
:

(C.77)

\varphi(t,x,\xi)=H(t,x,\xi)\xi-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+\xi^{2}}\cdot\lambda\circ H(t,x,\xi)}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2}\circ F\big(s,H(t,x,\xi),\xi\big)ds.

We now prove part (ii):

Lemma C.3
.

The solution
\varphi
to (
C.69
) satisfies the bound (
C.70
).

Proof.

1. We define
{\widetilde{\varphi}}\in C^{\infty}\big((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)\big)
by

(C.78)

{\widetilde{\varphi}}(t,x,\zeta)={\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)\tan\zeta-\dfrac{\cos\zeta}{\lambda\circ H(t,x,\zeta)}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2}\circ{\widetilde{F}}\big(s,{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta),\zeta\big)ds.

The heuristic behind this definition comes from the relations (
C.58
) between
F,{\widetilde{F}},H
and
{\widetilde{H}}
. They connect
\varphi
and
{\widetilde{\varphi}}
through

(C.79)

\varphi(t,x,\xi)={\widetilde{\varphi}}(t,x,\arctan\xi),\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in X.

2. We focus on the first term in (
C.78
). Using
{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\pi/2)=F_{-}(t,x)
, we obtain:

(C.80)

\begin{gathered}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)\tan\zeta={\widetilde{H}}\left(t,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\tan\zeta+\left({\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)-{\widetilde{H}}\left(t,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\right)\tan\zeta=F_{-}(t,x)\tan\zeta+\psi(t,x,\zeta),\\
\qquad\psi(t,x,\zeta)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \left({\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)-F_{-}(t,x)\right)\tan\zeta=\left(\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\tan\zeta\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{\partial{\widetilde{H}}}{\partial\zeta}\left(s,x,{\theta}\zeta+(1-{\theta})\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)d{\theta}.\end{gathered}

The identities defining
\psi
show that it is a function on
(-T,T)\times\mathbb{R}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
that extends smoothly to
(-T,T)\times\mathbb{R}\times(-\pi/2,3\pi/2)
. Moreover, since
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}
is uniformly bounded on
\Omega=\Omega_{0}\times\mathbb{R}
,
\psi
and all its derivatives are uniformly bounded on e.g.
\Omega_{0}\times(0,\pi)
.

3. The second term in (
C.78
) defines a function in
C^{\infty}_{b}(X)
, because
\lambda\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and all derivatives of
{\widetilde{F}},{\widetilde{H}}
are uniformly bounded on
X
. Thanks to Step 2 and the relation (
C.79
), we deduce that we can we write

(C.81)

{\widetilde{\varphi}}(t,x,\zeta)=F_{+}(t,x)\tan\zeta+R(t,x,\zeta),\qquad\varphi(t,x,\xi)=F_{-}(t,x)\xi+R(t,x,\arctan\xi),

where
R\in C^{\infty}_{b}\big(\Omega_{0}\times(0,\pi)\big)
.
To prove the bound (
C.70
), we write, thanks to (
C.81
):

(C.82)

\varphi(t,x,\xi)-x\xi=\xi\big(F_{-}(t,x)-x\big)+R(t,x,\arctan\xi)=\xi\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\partial{\widetilde{F}}(s,x,\pi/2)}{\partial s}ds+R(t,x,\arctan\xi).

The above integral defines a function in
C^{\infty}_{b}(\Omega_{0})
because derivatives of
{\widetilde{F}}
are bounded, see Lemma
C.1
. Moreover
R\in C^{\infty}_{b}\big(\Omega_{0}\times(0,\pi)\big)
, therefore
R(t,x,\arctan\xi)
defines an expression in
C^{\infty}_{b}\big(\Omega_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\big)
. It follows that
\partial_{\xi}(\varphi(t,x,\xi)-x\xi)
satisfies the bound (
C.70
) for
(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
. A similar approach yields the result with
\mathbb{R}^{-}
replacing
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
∎

In Lemmas
C.4
-
C.5
below, we prove stationary and non-stationary estimates on
{\varphi}
. These will serve to prove localization and spreading estimates for superposition of WKB waves (see §
C.5
).

Lemma C.4
.

After potentially shrinking
T
, there exists
c>0
such that for
(t,x,\xi)\in X
:

(C.83)

\partial_{\xi}^{2}{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)\leq-ct\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3}.

Proof.

1. We will rely on the formula
\partial_{\xi}\varphi=H
. This comes from the fact that
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)
does not depend on
t
:

(C.84)

\begin{gathered}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)\big)=\dfrac{\partial^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial\xi\partial t}+\dot{x_{t}}\dfrac{\partial^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x\partial\xi}=-\dfrac{\partial\big(p(x,\partial_{x}\varphi)\big)}{\partial\xi}(t,x_{t},\xi)+\dot{x_{t}}\dfrac{\partial^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x\partial\xi}\\
=-\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{t},\xi_{t})\dfrac{\partial^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x\partial\xi}+\dot{x_{t}}\dfrac{\partial^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x\partial\xi}=0,\end{gathered}

where we used the eikonal equation and
\dot{x_{t}}=\partial_{\xi}p(x_{t},\xi_{t})
. We therefore deduce

(C.85)

\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)=\partial_{\xi}\varphi(0,x_{0},\xi)=x_{0}=H(t,x_{t},\xi),

and therefore
\partial_{\xi}\varphi=H
.

From this identity, we deduce that Lemma
C.4
is equivalent to
\partial_{\xi}H(t,x,\xi)\leq-ct\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3}
for
t>0
small enough and
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We observe moreover that

(C.86)

\partial_{\xi}H(t,x,\xi)=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\arctan\xi),

therefore (
C.83
) is equivalent to
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)\leq-ct\cos\zeta
for
t>0
small enough and
(x,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
.

2. Taking derivatives with respect to
\zeta
then
t
of the relation
F(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)={\operatorname{Id}}
, we obtain

(C.87)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)+\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}\cdot\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)=0,\\
\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)+\partial_{t}{\widetilde{H}}\cdot\partial_{x}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)+\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}\cdot\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)\\
+\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)+\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}\partial_{t}{\widetilde{H}}\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)=0.\end{gathered}

Because of
{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)={\widetilde{H}}(0,x,\zeta)=x
, we know that
\partial_{x}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}
and
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}
vanish at
t=0
, and
\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)=1
. Therefore, evaluating (
C.87
) at
t=0
produces

(C.88)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)+\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(0,x,\zeta)=0.\end{gathered}

3. We now compute
\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)=\partial_{\zeta}\dot{x_{t}}
with
{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\zeta)=\dot{x_{t}}
and
{\widetilde{G}}(t,x,\zeta)=\zeta_{t}
. Taking derivatives of (
C.56
) with respect to
\zeta
,

(C.89)

\partial_{\zeta}\dot{x_{t}}=\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t})\sin\zeta_{t}\cdot\partial_{\zeta}x_{t}+\lambda(x_{t})\cos\zeta_{t}\cdot\partial_{\zeta}\zeta_{t}.

Since
\partial_{\zeta}\zeta_{0}=1
and
\partial_{\zeta}x_{0}=0
, we conclude that
\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(0,x,\zeta)=\lambda(x)\cos\zeta
. From (
C.88
), we deduce that
\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(0,x,\zeta)=-\lambda(x)\cos\zeta
. Hence, thanks to Taylor’s formula and
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(X)
,

(C.90)

\left|\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)+t\lambda(x)\cos\zeta\right|\leq Ct^{2}.

This inequality shows that (
C.83
) is valid as long as
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\lambda\cdot\cos\zeta\geq 2Ct
.

4. We now study the situation for
\zeta
near
\pi/2
. We write Taylor’s inequality:

(C.91)

\left|\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)-\sum_{j=0}^{2}\partial_{\zeta}^{j+1}{\widetilde{H}}\left(t,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\left(\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)^{j}\right|\leq C\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|^{3}.

We note that
{\widetilde{G}}(t,x,\pi/2)=\pi/2
, therefore with
x_{t}={\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\pi/2)
we have from (
C.89
):

(C.92)

\partial_{\zeta}\dot{x_{t}}=\lambda^{\prime}(x_{t})\partial_{\zeta}x_{t},\qquad\partial_{\zeta}x_{0}=0.

It follows that
\partial_{\zeta}x_{t}=0
for every
t
; that is,
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{F}}(t,x,\pi/2)=0
. From the first identity in (
C.87
) and
\partial_{x}{\widetilde{F}}(t,{\widetilde{H}},\zeta)=1+O(t)
, we deduce that
\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\pi/2)=0
for
t
sufficiently small. Taking derivatives of
\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(0,x,\zeta)=\lambda(x)\cos\zeta
with respect to
\zeta
, we obtain

(C.93)

\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}^{2}{\widetilde{H}}\left(0,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=\lambda(x),\qquad\partial_{t}\partial_{\zeta}^{3}{\widetilde{H}}\left(0,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=0.

Therefore, using that
\partial_{\zeta}^{j}H(0,x,\zeta)=0
for
j\geq 1
, we deduce that

(C.94)

\left|\partial_{\zeta}^{2}{\widetilde{H}}\left(t,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)-\lambda(x)t\right|\leq Ct^{2},\qquad\left|\partial_{\zeta}^{3}{\widetilde{H}}\left(t,x,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\right|\leq Ct^{2}.

Plugging these in (
C.91
), we deduce that

(C.95)

\left|\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)-\lambda(x)t\left(\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\right|\leq C\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|^{3}+Ct^{2}\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|+Ct^{2}\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|^{2}\leq C\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|^{3}+Ct^{2}\left|\zeta-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right|.

It follows that for
(t,x,\zeta)\in X_{0}\times(0,\pi)
,

(C.96)

\left|\partial_{\zeta}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x,\zeta)+\lambda(x)t\cos\zeta\right|\leq C\cos\zeta\left(\cos^{2}\zeta+t^{2}\right).

This inequality shows that (
C.83
) is valid as long as
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\lambda\cdot t\geq C\left(\cos^{2}\zeta+t^{2}\right)
, that is
t
sufficiently small and
t\geq C\cos^{2}\zeta
. This region of validity combined with that identified in Step 3 cover a whole strip of times near
0
, see Figure
C.5
. This proves the lemma. ∎

Figure C.5.
Step 3 shows that (
C.83
) holds within the blue region. It therefore misses positive times for
\zeta
near
\pm\pi/2
. Step 4 shows that
(
C.83
) holds in the red-enclosed region, hence covers points
(t,\zeta)
missed by Step 3.

Lemma
C.3
provides the bound (
C.70
) and Lemma
C.5
provides the second estimate in (
C.71
). To complete the proof of Theorem
C.4
, it remains to show the first estimate in (
C.71
):

Lemma C.5
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that

(C.97)

\begin{gathered}\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)\big|\geq c\big(t\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2}+{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\big),\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in\Omega,\qquad x\notin I_{t}.\end{gathered}

Proof.

We assume that
x\geq x_{t}^{+}
; the case
x\leq x_{t}^{-}
is treated similarly. We first note that

(C.98)

\lim_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},\xi)=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}H(t,x_{t}^{+},\xi)=\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\pi/2}{\widetilde{H}}(t,x_{t}^{\pm},\zeta)={\widetilde{H}}\big(t,x_{t}^{+},\pi/2\big)=0.

Thanks to (
C.98
):

(C.99)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)-\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},\xi)+\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},\xi)\\
=\int_{x_{t}^{+}}^{x}\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,s,\xi)ds-\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},s)ds.\end{gathered}

We note that
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi(0,s,\xi)=1
and
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\Omega)
, hence
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,s,\xi)=1+O(t)
for
t
sufficiently small, uniformly in
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For the term
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,x,s)
, we use Lemma
C.4
. We deduce that for
t
sufficiently small,

(C.100)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)\geq(x-x_{t}^{+})\big(1+O(t)\big)+ct\int_{\xi}^{\infty}\left\langle s\right\rangle^{-3}ds\geq c\left(x-x_{t}+\dfrac{t}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2}}\right).\end{gathered}

This proves (
C.97
) when
x\geq x_{t}^{+}
. When
x\leq x_{t}^{-}
instead, we use the identity

(C.101)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=\int_{x_{t}^{-}}^{x}\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,s,\xi)ds+\int_{-\infty}^{\xi}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},s)ds,\end{gathered}

and a similar reasoning. This completes the proof. ∎

The next result shows that
I_{t}
is the smallest possible set such that (
C.97
) holds. Standard techniques imply that superpositions of WKB waves are essentially supported within the cone
\{(t,x):x\in I_{t}\}
; see §
C.5
.

Corollary C.3
.

For
t\in(0,T)
and
x\in I_{t}=(x_{t}^{-},x_{t}^{+})
, the equation
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=0
has a unique solution denoted
\Xi(t,x)
. It depends smoothly on
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
; moreover, there exists
c>0
such that

(C.102)

\big|\Xi(t,x)\big|\geq c\left|\dfrac{t}{x-x_{t}^{\pm}}\right|^{1/2},\qquad t\in(0,T),\quad x\in I_{t}.

Proof.

Since
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\cdot)
is decreasing (see Lemma
C.4
), the equation
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=0
has a unique solution. Moreover, using that
\partial_{\xi}\varphi=H
, the equation
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=0
is equivalent to
x=F(t,0,\xi)
. But for
x\in I_{t}
:

(C.103)

\lim_{\xi\rightarrow-\infty}F(t,0,\xi)=x_{t}^{-}<x<x_{t}^{+}=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}F(t,0,\xi).

The intermediate value theorem produces then a solution
\Xi(t,x)
to
x=F(t,0,\xi)
, which is unique. Smoothness with respect to
(t,x)
follows from the implicit function theorem, noting that
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big)\neq 0
.

It remains to prove (
C.102
). This is a statement about the behavior of
\Xi(t,x)
as
x
approaches
x_{t}^{\pm}
. we use (
C.99
) specialized at
\xi=\Xi(t,x)
:

(C.104)

\begin{gathered}0=\partial_{\xi}\varphi\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big)=\int_{x_{t}^{+}}^{x}\partial^{2}_{x\xi}\varphi\big(t,s,\Xi(t,x)\big)ds-\int_{\Xi(t,x)}^{+\infty}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(t,x_{t}^{+},s)ds\\
\geq\int_{x_{t}^{+}}^{x}\partial^{2}_{x\xi}\varphi\big(t,s,\Xi(t,x)\big)ds+ct\Xi(t,x)^{-2}.\end{gathered}

where we used Lemma
C.4
for the lower bound.
In particular, using that
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\varphi
is uniformly bounded:

(C.105)

C(x-x_{t}^{+})\geq\int_{x}^{x_{t}^{+}}\partial^{2}_{x\xi}\varphi\big(t,s,\Xi(t,x)\big)ds\geq ct\Xi(t,x)^{-2};\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad\Xi(t,x)\geq c\sqrt{\dfrac{t}{x_{t}^{+}-x}}.

A similar estimate holds as
x\rightarrow x_{t}^{-}
.∎

Later, we will need bound on the function
\vartheta
defined by:

(C.106)

\vartheta(t,x)=\varphi\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big),\qquad t\in[0,T],\qquad x\in I_{t}.

Lemma C.6
.

There exists
C>0
such that for
j\in[1,3]
:

(C.107)

\big|\partial_{x}^{j}\vartheta(t,x)\big|\leq C{\operatorname{d}}(x,\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{t})^{-3j},\qquad t\in[0,T],\qquad x\in I_{t}.

Proof.

1. Since
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\Xi)=0
, we have
\partial_{x}\vartheta(t,x)=\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x,\Xi)
. Taking then successive derivatives with respect to
x
of this expression, noting that
|\partial_{x}^{j}\varphi(t,x,\xi)|\leq C\left\langle\xi\right\rangle
and
\partial_{\xi}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\ell}\varphi
is uniformly bounded for
k\geq 1
produces the inequalities:

(C.108)

\big|\partial_{x}\vartheta\big|\leq C\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle,\qquad\big|\partial_{x}^{2}\vartheta\big|\leq C\big(\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial_{x}\Xi\right\rangle\big),\qquad\big|\partial_{x}^{3}\vartheta\big|\leq C\big(\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial_{x}\Xi\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle\partial_{x}^{2}\Xi\right\rangle\big).

It remains now to bound
\Xi
and its derivatives.

2. Taking derivatives of the expression
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\Xi)=0
with respect to
\xi
, we obtain – with all identities evaluated at
\xi=\Xi(t,x)
:

(C.109)

\partial_{x}\Xi\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi+\partial_{x}\varphi=0,\qquad\partial_{x}^{2}\Xi\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi+(\partial_{x}\Xi)^{2}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{3}\varphi+\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi+\partial_{x}\Xi\cdot\partial^{2}_{x\xi}\varphi=0.

Using the lower bound on
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi
(Lemma
C.4
) and the upper bounds on
\partial^{\alpha}\varphi
(Lemma
C.3
), we deduce that

(C.110)

\big|\partial_{x}\Xi\big|\leq C\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{3},\qquad\big|\partial_{x}^{2}\Xi\big|\leq\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{8}.

Plugging the bounds (
C.110
) in (
C.108
), we deduce that

(C.111)

\big|\partial_{x}^{j}\vartheta\big|\leq C\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{3j},\qquad j\in[1,3].

3. We now prove an upper bound on
\Xi
. We recall that
\partial_{\xi}\varphi=H
, hence
H(t,x,\Xi)=0
. It follows that

(C.112)

H_{+}(t,x)=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}H(t,x,\xi)-H(t,x,\Xi)=\int_{\Xi(t,x)}^{+\infty}\partial_{\xi}H(t,x,s)ds.

Moreover,
\partial_{\xi}H=\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2}\partial_{\xi}{\widetilde{H}}
, and
\partial_{\xi}{\widetilde{H}}
is uniformly bounded:
{\widetilde{H}}
is the reciprocal of
{\widetilde{F}}
, whose derivative with respect to
x
is bounded below, see Lemmas
C.1
-
C.2
. We deduce that when
\Xi>0
,

(C.113)

\big|H_{+}(t,x)\big|\leq C\int_{\Xi}^{+\infty}s^{-2}ds=\dfrac{C}{\Xi},

therefore
\Xi(t,x)\leq C\big|H_{+}(t,x)\big|^{-1}
. We likewise have
-\Xi(t,x)\geq C\big|H_{-}(t,x)\big|^{-1}
when
\Xi(t,x)<0
. Therefore an upper bound on
\Xi
follows from a lower bound on
H_{+}
and
H_{-}
. Thanks to Lemma
C.1
and (
C.67
),
\partial_{x}{\widetilde{H}}
hence
\partial_{x}H
are bounded below on
\Omega
, hence

(C.114)

H_{+}(t,x)=H_{+}(t,x)-H_{+}(t,x_{t}^{+})=\int^{x}_{x_{t}^{+}}\partial_{x}H_{+}(t,y)dy\geq c(x_{t}^{+}-x).

A similar bound holds for
\partial_{x}H_{-}
. We conclude that
|\Xi(t,x)|\leq C{\operatorname{d}}(x,\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{t})^{-1}
, which plugged in (
C.111
) completes the proof.
∎

C.4.4.
Transport equation

We focus now on solving the transport equation that arises from the strategy outlined in §
C.4.1
:

(C.115)

\left\langle L_{1}\alpha u,u\right\rangle=0,\qquad L_{1}=D_{t}+\left(\lambda(x)D_{x}+\dfrac{\lambda^{\prime}(x)}{2i}\right)\sigma_{1}+2n\mu(x)\partial_{x}\varphi\big(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi\big)+s(x).

In (
C.115
),
u
is a real-valued normalized eigenvector
u
of the matrix
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi
, for the eigenvalue
\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle
; specifically:

(C.116)

u=\left[\begin{matrix}\cos({\theta}/2)\\
\sin({\theta}/2)\end{matrix}\right],\qquad{\theta}=\dfrac{\pi}{2}-\arctan\partial_{x}\varphi.

Lemma C.7
.

The solution to (
C.115
) with initial data
\alpha(0,x,\xi)=1
is

(C.117)

\alpha=\big|\partial_{x}H\big|^{1/2}\cdot\exp\left(-2ni\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2}\cdot\lambda^{2}\circ H\cdot\int_{H}^{x}\dfrac{\mu}{\lambda^{3}}-\int_{0}^{t}s\circ F(\tau,H,\xi)d\tau\right),

with all functions in (
C.117
) evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)
. In particular,
\alpha
is an admissible function of order
0
(see Definition
C.1
).

Proof.

1. Since
|u|^{2}=1
, we have
\left\langle\partial_{t}u,u\right\rangle=0
. Moreover, we observe that
\partial_{x}\left\langle\sigma_{3}u,u\right\rangle=2\left\langle\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}u,u\right\rangle
. Also, since
u
is normalized eigenvector of
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi
for the eigenvalue
\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle
,

(C.118)

\begin{gathered}\left\langle\big(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi\big)u,u\right\rangle=\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle,\\
\left\langle\sigma_{3}u,u\right\rangle=\dfrac{\left\langle\sigma_{3}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi)u,u\right\rangle}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle}=\dfrac{\left\langle\sigma_{3}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{3}\partial_{x}\varphi)u,u\right\rangle}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle}=\dfrac{\partial_{x}\varphi}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle},\end{gathered}

where in the second identity we used that
\sigma_{3}^{2}={\operatorname{Id}}
and
\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}
is antisymmetric.

2. Thanks to the relations (
C.118
), we deduce that, with all functions evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)
:

(C.119)

\begin{gathered}i\left\langle L_{1}\alpha u,u\right\rangle=\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}+\nu\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}+is+\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{\partial\nu}{\partial x}+2ni\mu\partial_{x}\varphi\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle\right)\alpha,\qquad\nu\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{\lambda\partial_{x}\varphi}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle}.\end{gathered}

3. We now solve the equation (
C.119
) along the bicharacteristic of
H_{p}
(i.e. the projection along the
x
-axis of the Hamiltonian trajectories of
p
). We remark that

(C.120)

\nu(t,x_{t},\xi)=\dfrac{\lambda(x_{t})\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x_{t},\xi)\right\rangle}=\dfrac{\lambda(x_{t})\xi_{t}}{\left\langle\xi_{t}\right\rangle}=\dot{x_{t}}.

Thanks to (
C.119
), we deduce that
\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)
satisfies the linear, homogeneous first order ODE

(C.121)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dt}\big(\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)\big)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial t}+\dot{x_{t}}\dfrac{\partial\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x}=\dfrac{\partial\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial t}+\nu(t,x_{t},\xi)\dfrac{\partial\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)}{\partial x}

(C.122)

\displaystyle=-\left(is(x_{t})+\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{\partial\nu}{\partial x}(t,x_{t},\xi)+2ni\mu(x_{t})\xi_{t}\left\langle\xi_{t}\right\rangle\right)\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi).

To solve this equation, we first compute an antiderivative of
\partial_{x}\nu(t,x_{t},\xi)
. Thanks to
\nu(t,x_{t},\xi_{t})=\dot{x_{t}}=\partial_{t}F(t,x,\xi)
and
H(t,x_{t},\xi)=x
, we have, with all expressions evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)
:

(C.123)

\dfrac{\partial\nu}{\partial x}=\left(\dfrac{\partial H}{\partial x}\cdot\dfrac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial x\partial t}\right)\big(t,H,\xi\big)=\left(\dfrac{1}{\partial_{x}F}\cdot\dfrac{\partial\partial_{x}F}{\partial t}\right)\big(t,H,\xi\big)=\dfrac{\partial\ln|\partial_{x}F|}{\partial t}(t,H,\xi),

where we used that
\partial_{x}H\cdot\partial_{x}F(t,H,\xi)=1
. It follows that

(C.124)

\dfrac{\partial\nu}{\partial x}(t,x_{t},\xi)=\dfrac{\partial\ln|\partial_{x}F|}{\partial t}(t,x,\xi).

We now focus on the term
\mu(x_{t})\xi_{t}\left\langle\xi_{t}\right\rangle
from (
C.122
). Thanks to
p(x_{t},\xi_{t})=p(x,\xi)=\lambda(x)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle
:

(C.125)

\mu(x_{t})\xi_{t}\left\langle\xi_{t}\right\rangle=\dfrac{\dot{x_{t}}\mu(x_{t})\left\langle\xi_{t}\right\rangle^{2}}{\lambda(x_{t})}=\dfrac{\dot{x_{t}}\mu(x_{t})}{\lambda(x_{t})^{3}}p(x,\xi)^{2}=p(x,\xi)^{2}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\int_{x}^{x_{t}}\dfrac{\mu}{\lambda^{3}}\right).

4. We now integrate the ODE (
C.122
), using the formulas (
C.124
) and (
C.125
):

(C.126)

\alpha(t,x_{t},\xi)=\exp\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\ln\big|\partial_{x}F(t,x,\xi)\big|-2nip(x,\xi)^{2}\cdot\int_{x}^{x_{t}}\dfrac{\mu}{\lambda^{3}}-i\int_{0}^{t}s(x_{\tau})d\tau\right).

Using that
x=H(t,x_{t},\xi)
, and using
\partial_{x}H\cdot\partial_{x}F(t,H,\xi)=1
, we conclude that

(C.127)

\alpha=\big|\partial_{x}H\big|^{1/2}\cdot\exp\left(-2ni\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2}\cdot\lambda^{2}\circ H\cdot\int_{H}^{x}\dfrac{\mu}{\lambda^{3}}-i\int_{0}^{t}s\circ F(\tau,H,\xi)d\tau\right),

with all functions evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)
. This completes the proof of (
C.117
).

5. It remains to show that
\alpha
is an admissible function of order
0
. The term involving
s
does not cause problem because derivatives of
F
and
H
are uniformly bounded. By construction of
T
, the function
\partial_{x}H
is in
C^{\infty}_{b}(X)
and is bounded below by a positive constant – see Lemma
C.2
. Therefore
\big|\partial_{x}H(t,x,\xi)\big|^{1/2}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(X)
, hence it is admissible.

We now look at the factor

(C.128)

\exp\left(-2ni\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2}\cdot\gamma(t,x,\xi)\right),\qquad\gamma(t,x,\xi)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \lambda^{2}\circ H(t,x,\xi)\cdot\int_{H(t,x,\xi)}^{x}\dfrac{\mu}{\lambda^{3}}

arising in (
C.127
). We note that
H(t,x,\xi)-x
is bounded together with all its derivatives on
X
; and so are
\lambda^{2}\circ H
and
\mu\lambda^{-3}
. Therefore
\gamma\in C^{\infty}_{b}(X)
. Taking
k
derivatives of (
C.128
) produces then growth at most
\left\langle n,\xi^{2}\right\rangle^{k}
. Therefore (
C.128
), hence
\alpha
, are admissible of order
0
.
∎

We then set
b_{0}=\alpha u
, where
\alpha
is given by (
C.117
), so that
L_{1}b_{0}
is orthogonal to the kernel of
L_{0}
. The equation
L_{0}b_{1}+L_{1}b_{0}=0
has then a unique solution
b_{1}\in(\ker L_{0})^{\perp}
. We then set

(C.129)

b=b_{0}+\epsilon b_{1}

Lemma C.8
.

The function
b
is admissible of order
1
.

Proof.

Since
b=b_{0}+\epsilon b_{1}=\alpha u+\epsilon b_{1}
, and
\alpha
is admissible of order
0
(see Lemma
C.7
), it suffices to show that
u
and
b_{1}
are both admissible of order (at most)
1
. Since
L_{0}
is a
2\times 2
matrix with eigenvalues
0
and
2\partial_{t}\varphi=-2\lambda\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle
, we have

(C.130)

b_{1}=\dfrac{L_{1}(\alpha u)}{2\lambda\left\langle\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle}.

The estimate (
C.70
) implies that
\partial_{x}\varphi
is admissible of order
1/2
(much better, in fact); hence from (
C.116
),
u
is admissible of order
0
. The coefficients in the non-differential parts of
L_{1}
are admissible of order
3/2
, while the differential coefficients are just
C^{\infty}_{b}
, see (
C.115
). Hence
L_{1}(\alpha u)
is admissible of order
3/2
. Moreover, since
\partial_{x}\varphi(0,x,\xi)=\xi
, we have from (
C.70
) that

(C.131)

\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x,\xi)=\xi+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{tx}^{2}\varphi(\tau,x,\xi)d\tau\geq\left(1-Ct\right)\xi.

From the expression (
C.130
) of
b_{1}
, we deduce that
b_{1}
is admissible of order
1
. Therefore
b
is admissible of order
1
.
∎

Lemma C.9
.

With
b
and
\varphi
defined as above, we have

(C.132)

\big(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\big)be^{i\varphi/\epsilon}=\epsilon^{2}re^{i\varphi/\epsilon},

for an admissible function
r
of order
4
.

Proof.

We recall that

(C.133)

e^{-i\varphi/\epsilon}\big(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\big)e^{i\varphi/\epsilon}=L_{0}+\epsilon L_{1}+\epsilon^{2}L_{2}

where
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2}
are given in (
C.49
). Using that
b=b_{0}+\epsilon b_{1}
,
L_{0}b_{0}=0
and
L_{1}b_{0}+L_{1}b_{0}=0
, we deduce that

(C.134)

\big(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\big)be^{i\varphi/\epsilon}=\epsilon^{2}\big(L_{2}b+L_{1}b_{1}\big)e^{i\varphi/\epsilon},

and therefore the function
r
emerging in (
C.49
) is given by
r=L_{2}b+L_{1}b_{1}
