. We use the formulas (
C.117
) and the fact that
b,b_{1}
are admissible of order
0
to check that
r
is admissible and compute its order. The worst term is
2n\partial_{x}\varphi\mu(x)D_{x}b
, which is admissible of order
3/2+1+1=7/2\leq 4
. This completes the proof.
∎

C.4.5.
Conter-propagating WKB wave

We briefly explain how to construct a WKB wave starting from the other branch of (
C.51
). One can check that the WKB solution constructed in §
C.4.3
is also defined for short negative time, and satisfies the same estimates; in fact, one has

(C.135)

{\varphi}(t,x,\xi)=-{\varphi}(-t,x,-\xi).

Consider then, for
t<0
:

(C.136)

\mathcal{T}(be^{i\varphi/\epsilon})(t,x,\xi)=i\sigma_{2}{\overline{b(t,x,\xi)}}e^{-i\varphi(t,x,\xi)}.

Because
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
anticommutes with
\mathcal{T}
, see §
C.2.3
, (
C.136
) is another WKB solution for
(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon})
. It has orientation orthogonal to
be^{i\varphi}
. We refer to it as the counter-propagating WKB wave to
be^{i\varphi}
.

C.5.
Parametrix

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
C.1
. Our proof strategy goes as follows:

1.

We produce a parametrix
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
for the operator
\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
. It takes the form of an integral superposition of the WKB waves constructed in §
C.4
.

2.

We prove an asymptotic expansion,
L^{\infty}
, and
L^{2}
estimates for
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

3.

We sum the parametrices, asymptotic expansions and estimates for each component in the block-diagonal decomposition of
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
(see §
C.5.3
).

The result is a parametrix for
hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}
, with associated
L^{2}/L^{\infty}
estimates that imply Theorem
C.1
.

C.5.1.
Preliminary estimates

We start by studying integral quantities of the form

(C.137)

Q(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi(t,x,\xi)/\epsilon}\cdot q(t,x,\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{\sqrt{\epsilon}},

where
q
is an admissible function of order
-\infty
, see Definition
C.1
. Quantities of the form (
C.137
) are typically superposition of WKB waves constructed in §
C.4
. We prove here that (
C.137
) expands in terms of the critical point
\Xi(t,x)
of
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\cdot)
.

Before stating our next result, we comment that while technically speaking, the quantity
q\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big)
is only defined for
x\in I_{t}
, it extends smoothly for
x\in\mathbb{R}
by:

(C.138)

q\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big)=0,\qquad x\notin I_{t}.

This is a consequence of the rapid decay of
\xi\mapsto q(t,x,\xi)
(
q
is an admissible function of order
-\infty
) and of the bound (
C.102
) for
\Xi(t,x)
as
x
approaches
x_{t}^{\pm}
.

Theorem C.5
.

If
q
is admissible of order
-\infty
, then:

(i)

Uniformly for
t
in compact subsets of
(0,T)
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(C.139)

Q(t,x)=\left(e^{i\varphi/\epsilon}\sqrt{\dfrac{2\pi i}{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot q\right)\big(t,x,\Xi(t,x)\big)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big({\varepsilon}^{1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).

(ii)

Uniformly for
t\in(0,T)
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
n\geq 1
:

(C.140)

Q(t,x)=O_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\big(t^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}\big),\qquad Q(t,x)=O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(t^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).

Proof of (
C.139
).

1. In this proof we will occasionally drop the variables
t,x
to keep equations under reasonable length. Assume that
(t,x)
are such that
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\cdot)
does not vanish on the support of
q(t,x,\cdot)
. We integrate (
C.137
) by parts:

(C.141)

Q(t,x)=i\epsilon^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi/\epsilon}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\dfrac{q}{\partial_{\xi}\varphi}d\xi.

Using that
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi
is uniformly bounded above, we deduce there exists
C>0
such that for every
(t,x)
with
\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t,x,\cdot)
not vanishing on the support of
q(t,x,\cdot)
:

(C.142)

\big|Q(t,x)\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{\xi}\varphi|^{j-2}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi.

2. If
x\notin I_{t}
then according to Lemma
C.5
:

(C.143)

\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\xi)\big|\geq c\big(t\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2}+{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\big).

We then apply (
C.142
):

(C.144)

\big|Q(t,x)\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{1}\big(t+{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\big)^{j-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4-2j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi,\qquad x\notin I_{t}.

By assumption
q
and its derivatives decay faster than any polynomial in
\xi
, uniformly for
(t,x)\in\Omega_{0}
. Therefore, we deduce that

(C.145)

\int_{x\notin I_{t}}\big|Q(t,x)\big|^{2}dx\leq C\epsilon\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{x\notin I_{t}}\big(t+{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\big)^{2j-4}dx\leq Ct^{-3}\epsilon.

3. We now work with
x\in I_{t}
. Set
C_{0}=ct(2+2|\partial_{\xi}^{3}\varphi|_{\infty})^{-1}
, where
c
is the constant involved in (
C.71
), and assume that

(C.146)

{\operatorname{supp}}\ q(t,x,\cdot)\subset\big\{\xi:\ |\xi-\Xi(t,x)|\geq C_{0}\left\langle\Xi(t,x)\right\rangle^{-3}\big\}.

Then on the support of
q(t,x,\cdot)
, we have (dropping the dependence in
t,x
):

(C.147)

\begin{gathered}\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\xi)\big|=\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\xi)-\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\Xi)\big|=\left|\int_{\Xi}^{\xi}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\theta)d\theta\right|\geq ct\left|\int_{\Xi}^{\xi}\left\langle\theta\right\rangle^{-3}d\theta\right|.\end{gathered}

Assume first that
(t,x)
are such that
C_{0}\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{-3}\geq 2
. Then the interval between
\xi
and
\Xi
contains either
[\xi-2,\xi]
or
[\xi,\xi+2]
. In particular, using that
\left\langle\xi-2\right\rangle,\left\langle\xi\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\xi+2\right\rangle
are comparable, we deduce that

(C.148)

\begin{gathered}\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\xi)\big|\geq ct\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3},\qquad(t,x,\xi)\in{\operatorname{supp}}(q).\end{gathered}

Applying (
C.142
) produces

(C.149)

\big|Q(t,x)\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{6-3j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi.

Assume now that
(t,x)
are such that
C_{0}\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{-3}\leq 2
. Then if
\xi
is such that
|\xi-\Xi(t,x)|\geq C_{0}\left\langle\Xi(t,x)\right\rangle^{-3}
, then
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle
are comparable. It follows from (
C.140
) that

(C.150)

\big|\partial_{\xi}\varphi(\xi)\big|\geq ct|\xi-\Xi|\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3}\geq ct\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{-3}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-3}\geq ct\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-6}.

Again, applying (
C.142
) produces

(C.151)

\big|Q(t,x)\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{12-6j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi.

4. We now assume that
x\in I_{t}
, and that
(t,x)
are such that

(C.152)

{\operatorname{supp}}\ q(t,x,\cdot)\subset\{\xi:\ |\xi-\Xi(t,x)|\leq 2C_{0}\left\langle\Xi(t,x)\right\rangle^{-3}\}.

Following Hörmander’s approach to stationary phase estimates – see
[
H90
, §7.7]
– we write (again dropping the dependence in
t,x
to shorten expressions):

(C.153)

\begin{gathered}\varphi(\xi)=\varphi(\Xi)+\dfrac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}{2}(\xi-\Xi)^{2}+r(\xi)(\xi-\Xi)^{3},\qquad r(\xi)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{(1-{\theta})^{2}}{2}\partial_{\xi}^{3}\varphi\big({\theta}\xi+(1-{\theta})\Xi\big)d\theta.\\
\varphi_{\theta}(\xi)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \varphi(\Xi)+\dfrac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}{2}(\xi-\Xi)^{2}+{\theta}r(\xi)(\xi-\Xi)^{3},\qquad I({\theta})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}q\dfrac{d\xi}{\epsilon^{1/2}}.\end{gathered}

We note that
\varphi_{1}=\varphi
, in particular

(C.154)

Q(t,x)=I(1)=I(0)+\int_{0}^{1}I^{\prime}({\theta})d{\theta},\qquad I^{\prime}({\theta})=\dfrac{1}{h^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi-\Xi)^{3}e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}rqd\xi.

We show that
I^{\prime}({\theta})=O(\epsilon^{1/2})
. For that, we note that

(C.155)

\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{\theta}(\xi)=(\xi-\Xi){\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}(\xi),\qquad{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}(\xi)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)+{\theta}(\xi-\Xi)\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\xi}^{3}\varphi\big({\theta}^{\prime}\xi+(1-{\theta}^{\prime})\Xi\big)d\theta^{\prime}.

In particular,

(C.156)

\big|{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}(\xi)\big|\geq\big|\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)\big|-\dfrac{1}{2}|\partial_{\xi}^{3}\varphi|_{\infty}|\xi-\Xi|\geq\dfrac{1}{2}ct\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{-3},

where we used
|\xi-\Xi|\leq 2C_{0}\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{-3}
and the above value of
C_{0}
. We now integrate by parts twice the expression for
I^{\prime}(s)
using the expression

(C.157)

(\xi-\Xi)e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}=\dfrac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{\theta}}{{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}=\dfrac{\epsilon}{i{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}\dfrac{\partial e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}}{\partial\xi}.

This gives:

(C.158)

\begin{gathered}I^{\prime}({\theta})=\dfrac{1}{h^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{1}{i{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}\dfrac{\partial e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}}{\partial\xi}\cdot(\xi-\Xi)^{2}rqd\xi=\dfrac{i}{h^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}\cdot\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\dfrac{(\xi-\Xi)^{2}rq}{{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}d\xi\\
=\dfrac{i}{h^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi-\Xi)e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}\cdot\left(2+(\xi-\Xi)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\right)\dfrac{rq}{{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}d\xi=-\epsilon^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi_{\theta}/\epsilon}\cdot\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\dfrac{1}{{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}\left(2+(\xi-\Xi)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\right)\dfrac{rq}{{\widetilde{r}}_{\theta}}d\xi.\end{gathered}

Thanks to the bound (
C.156
), and that
\xi
and
\Xi
are comparable, we conclude that

(C.159)

\big|I^{\prime}({\theta})\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{12-3j}\big|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q\big|d\xi.

It remains to evaluate
I(0)
. We follow the standard calculation for oscillatory integrals with quadratic phase. From Plancherel’s formula:

(C.160)

\begin{gathered}I(0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi_{0}/\epsilon}q\dfrac{d\xi}{\epsilon^{1/2}}=e^{i\varphi(\Xi)/\epsilon}\sqrt{\dfrac{2\pi i}{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\epsilon\frac{y^{2}}{2\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}}e^{i\Xi y}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.41145pt\hbox{$q$}\hskip-2.41145pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raiseb =e^{i\varphi(\Xi)/\epsilon}\sqrt{\dfrac{2\pi i}{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}}\cdot\left(q(\Xi)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(e^{-i\epsilon\frac{y^{2}}{2\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)}}-1\Big)e^{i\Xi y}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt}(y)dy\right).\end{gathered}

We control the integral emerging in the right-hand side by:

(C.161)

\begin{gathered}\epsilon^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\epsilon|y|}{\big|2\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)\big|^{1/2}}\left|\hbox{\hskip ox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}(y)\right|dy\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|y|}{\big|2\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi(\Xi)\big|^{1/2}(1+y^{2})}\left|\big(q-\partial_{\xi}^{2}q\big)^{\hbox{\hskip 339.5451pt\hskip-2.04861pt\hbox{$\ $}\hskip-2.04861pt\hskip-339.5451pt\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.43054pt}{\resizebox{39915495}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.1425pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68599pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.48999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.48999pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.34999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-339.5451pt\hskip 339.5451pt}}(y)\right|dy\\
\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3}\big|\big(1-\partial_{\xi}^{2}\big)q\big|^{2}\right)^{1/2},\end{gathered}

where we use the Cauchy–Schwartz inequality, Plancherel’s formula and that
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle,\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle
are comparable.

5. To conclude, we first observe that when
x\notin I_{t}
, (
C.145
) provides
L^{2}
-decay. On the other hand, when
x\in I_{t}
,
\big|Q(t,x)\big|
is uniformly bounded. We then split the integral by mean of a cutoff function,

\chi\big(|\xi-\Xi|\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{3}\big)
, where
\chi
is even, smooth and has compact support, and
\chi(0)=1
. Taking at most two derivatives of
\chi
produces a factor
\left\langle\Xi\right\rangle^{6}
, which is comparable to
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{6}
on the support of
\chi^{\prime}
. Since
q
is of order
-\infty
,
L^{1}
-norms of
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{k}\partial_{\xi}^{j}q
decay faster than any power of
n
. This completes the proof of (
C.139
). ∎

Proof of the
L^{\infty}
-bound in (
C.140
).

To limit the length of expressions, we occasionally drop the variables
(t,x)
in the expressions below. We first assume that
q(t,x,\cdot)
has support in for
\xi\in[j,j+2]
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. According to Lemma
C.4
,
\varphi
satisfies the non-stationnary estimate

(C.162)

\left|\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\xi^{2}}\dfrac{\varphi(\xi)}{ct\left\langle j\right\rangle^{-3}}\right|\geq 1,\qquad\xi\in[j,j+2]

where
c
is independent of
j
and
t
. We can therefore apply
[
S93
, Corollary page 334]
. With the notations of this book, the large parameter is
\epsilon^{-1}ct\left\langle j\right\rangle^{-3}
and the constant involved is
c_{2}=5\cdot 2^{2-1}-2=8
, see the formula on
[
S93
, page 333]
. This implies that

(C.163)

\begin{gathered}\left|\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\int_{j}^{j+2}e^{i\varphi(\xi)/h}q(\xi)d\xi\right|\leq\dfrac{8}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\left(\dfrac{ct\left\langle j\right\rangle^{-3}}{\epsilon}\right)^{-1/2}\int_{j}^{j+2}\big|\partial_{\xi}q(\xi)\big|d\xi\leq\dfrac{C}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{j}^{j+2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3/2}\big|\partial_{\xi}q\big|d\xi,\end{gathered}

where we used that
\left\langle j\right\rangle^{3/2}
and
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3/2}
are comparable for
\xi\in[j,j+2]
. The constant
C
on the RHS is independent of
j,t,\epsilon
.

If now
q
has full support, we decompose it into pieces supported in
\xi\in[j,j+2]
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, and we sum the resulting estimates over
j
. It produces the bound

(C.164)

\begin{gathered}\left|\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\varphi(\xi)/h}q(\xi)d\xi\right|\leq\dfrac{C}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3/2}\big|\partial_{\xi}q\big|d\xi.\end{gathered}

Since
q
is admissible of order
-\infty
, the integral on the RHS decays faster than any power of
n
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of the
L^{2}
-bound in (
C.140
).

The bound (
C.144
) implies that

(C.165)

\big|Q(t,x)\big|\leq C\epsilon^{1/2}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{1}{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})^{j-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4-2j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi.

We square then integrate this expression for
{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\geq 1
. It gives

(C.166)

\int_{{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\geq 1}\big|Q(t,x)\big|^{2}dx\leq C\epsilon\left(\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4-2j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi\right)^{2}.

When
{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\leq 1
, we use the
L^{\infty}
bound and get – using that
|I_{t}|=O(t)
:

(C.167)

\int_{{\operatorname{d}}(x,I_{t})\leq 1}\big|Q(t,x)\big|^{2}dx\leq Ct^{-1}(t+1)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3/2}\big|\partial_{\xi}q\big|d\xi\right)^{2}\leq\dfrac{C}{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{3/2}\big|\partial_{\xi}q\big|d\xi\right)^{2}.

Summing (
C.166
) and (
C.167
) yields

(C.168)

\big|Q(t,\cdot)\big|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\dfrac{C}{t}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{4-2j}|\partial_{\xi}^{j}q|d\xi\right)^{2}.

Since
q
is admissible of order
-\infty
, the integral on the RHS decays faster than any power of
n
. This completes the proof of (
C.140
).
∎

C.5.2.
Parametrix

We now construct a parametrix for
\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}
as an integral operator of the form (
C.137
). Let
a_{n}
depending and
x
and
n
(but not on
\epsilon
) such that

(C.169)

(t,x,\xi)\mapsto{\widehat{a}}_{n}(\xi)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}a_{n}(x)dx

is admissible of order
-\infty
. Let
b_{n}^{+}=b
given by (
C.129
),
b_{n}^{-}=\ TTb=i\sigma_{2}\cdot{\overline{b}}
(note that
b
depends implicitly on
n
and
\epsilon
). Define then

(C.170)

\begin{gathered}\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}^{+}+\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}^{-},\qquad\text{where}\\
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}^{\pm}a_{n}(t,x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{2\pi\epsilon^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\pm i\varphi(t,x,\pm\xi)/\epsilon}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}(t,x,\xi)b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}(0,0,\xi)^{\top}{\widehat{a}}_{n}(\xi)d\xi.\end{gathered}

Note that
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}^{-}
is asssociated to the counter-propagating WKB wave constructed in §
C.4.5
. Moreover the integral (
C.170
) is of the form (
C.137
), in particular if
a
is an admissible function of order
-\infty
then uniformly in
t\in(0,T)
,
n\geq 1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
:

(C.171)

\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(t,\cdot)=O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(\epsilon n^{-\infty}t^{-1/2}).

Our next theorem shows that
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
is a parametrix for
\epsilon D_{t}+\not{D}_{n,\epsilon}
:
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a_{n}
approximately solves the equation

(C.172)

\left(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\right)f=0,\qquad f(0,x_{1})=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\cdot a_{n}\left(\dfrac{x}{\epsilon}\right).

Theorem C.6
.

The solution
f
to (
C.172
) satisfies, uniformly in
t\in(0,T)
,
n\geq 1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
:

(C.173)

\begin{gathered}f(t,x)=\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(t,x)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(\epsilon n^{-\infty}\big).\end{gathered}

Moreover, uniformly for
t
in compact subsets of
(0,T)
:

(C.174)

\begin{gathered}f(t,x)=\sum_{\pm}\left(e^{\pm i\varphi/\epsilon}\sqrt{\pm\dfrac{i}{2\pi\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}(0,0,\cdot)^{\top}{\widehat{a}}_{n}\right)\big(t,x,\pm\Xi(t,x)\big)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(\epsilon^{1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).\end{gathered}

The formula (
C.174
) implies that up to a small remainder,
f
is also essentially supported within the cone
\{(t,x):x\in I_{t}\}
and uniformly bounded as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. This is a particularly strong form of dispersion: the
L^{\infty}
-norm passes from
\epsilon^{-1/2}
at time
0
to
O(1)
as soon as
t>0
. The initial wavepacket structure is immediately lost.

Proof.

1. We first verify that
f(0,x)=\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(0,x)
. Using
\varphi(0,x,\xi)=x\xi
and that
b_{\pm}(0,x,\xi)
does not depend on
x
, see (
C.71
), we have

(C.175)

\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(0,x)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi\epsilon^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{ix\xi}{\epsilon}}\left(\sum_{\pm}b_{\pm}(0,0,\xi)\cdot b_{\pm}(0,0,\xi)^{\top}\right){\widehat{a}}(\xi)d\xi

Moreover,
b_{\pm}(0,0,\xi)
are orthonormal eigenvectors of
\sigma_{1}+\xi\sigma_{3}
. It follows that the matrix in (
C.175
) is the
2\times 2
identity, therefore

(C.176)

\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(0,x)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi\epsilon^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{ix\xi}{\epsilon}}{\widehat{a}}(\xi)d\xi=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\cdot a\left(\dfrac{x}{\epsilon}\right)=f(0,x).

2. Thanks to Theorem
C.3
, we have
\big(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\big)\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}=\epsilon^{2}R
, where

(C.177)

Ra(t,x)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi\epsilon^{1/2}}\sum_{\pm}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\pm i\varphi(t,x,\pm\xi)/\epsilon}r_{\pm}(t,x,\xi)b_{\pm}(0,0,\xi){\widehat{a}}(\xi)d\xi,

for two admissible functions
r_{\pm}
of order
4
. In particular, recalling that
{\widehat{a}}
is admissible of order
-\infty
,
Ra
is an integral of the form (
C.137
). From Theorem
C.5
(ii), we deduce that uniformly for
t\in(0,T)
:

(C.178)

\left\|\big(\epsilon D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\epsilon}\big)\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(t,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq Ct^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}\epsilon^{2}.

Moreover, the function
t\mapsto t^{-1/2}
is integrable near
0
. Applying Duhamel’s principle as in
[
BB+21
, Lemma 3.5]
, we conclude that

(C.179)

\big\|f(t,\cdot)-\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(t,\cdot)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\dfrac{Cn^{-\infty}}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\epsilon^{2}\tau^{-1/2}d\tau\leq Cn^{-\infty}t^{1/2}\epsilon.

This proves (
C.173
). To deduce (
C.174
), we observe that (
C.170
) precisely has the form of the integral studied in §
C.5.1
; therefore it suffices to apply Part (i) in Theorem
C.5
. This completes the proof. ∎

C.5.3.
Summability.

We now produce a leading order expansion for the solution to:

(C.180)

(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})F=0,\qquad F(0,x)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot A\left(\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{h}}\right),\qquad A\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Given the block-decomposition of
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
exhibited in §
C.2.2
, and Theorem
C.2
about traveling waves, a natural guess for the solution
F
to (
C.180
) is:

(C.181)

\begin{gathered}\mathfrak{E}A(t,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a_{n})(t,x_{1})G_{n,h}(x_{2}),\qquad\text{where:}\\
(\mathfrak{E}_{0}a_{0})(t,x_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{e^{iS_{t}}}{\sqrt{\rho_{t}h}}\cdot\left(\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{|4\pi\nu_{t}|^{1/2}}\cdot e^{i\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{4\nu_{t}}}\star a_{0}\right)\left(\dfrac{x_{1}-x_{t}^{+}}{\rho_{t}\sqrt{h}}\right);\qquad\text{and}\\
\mathfrak{E}_{n}\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}},\qquad h_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{h}{2n}},\qquad a_{n}(x_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{(2n)^{1/4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{2n}},x_{2}\right)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}.\end{gathered}

Theorem C.7
.

The solution
F
to (
C.180
) satisfies, uniformly for
t\in(0,T)
:

(C.182)

F(t,x)=\mathfrak{E}A(t,x)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\big(h^{1/2}\big).

Moreover, uniformly for
t
in compact sets of
(0,T)
:

(C.183)

\displaystyle F(t,x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{e^{iS_{t}}}{\sqrt{\rho_{t}h}}\cdot\left(\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{|4\pi\nu_{t}|^{1/2}}\cdot e^{i\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{4\nu_{t}}}\star a_{0}\right)\left(\dfrac{x_{1}-x_{t}^{+}}{\rho_{t}\sqrt{h}}\right)\cdot e^{-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2h}}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]

(C.184)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=1,\pm\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\left(e^{\pm i\varphi/h_{n}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\pm i}{2\pi\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(0,0,\cdot)^{\top}{\widehat{a}}_{n}\right)\big(t,x,\pm\Xi(t,x)\big)\cdot G_{n,h}(x_{2})+O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/4}\big).

Figure C.7.
Space-time diagram for propagation starting at
(0,0)
. The blue region represents all accessible positions. The red curve is the trajectory of a traveling mode. Away from this curve the propagation is dispersive.

Proof of Theorem
C.7
.

Thanks to Theorem
C.2
, it suffices to prove the theorem when
a_{0}=0
. We decompose the solution
F
to (
C.180
) as
F=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}
, where each
f_{n}
evolves according to
\left(h_{n}D_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,h_{n}}\right)f_{n}=0
. In particular, we have

(C.185)

\displaystyle f_{n}(0,x_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(0,x_{1},x_{2})G_{n,h}(x_{2})dx_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{1}{h^{3/4}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{h}},\dfrac{x_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)G_{n}\left(\dfrac{x_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)dx_{2}

(C.186)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{1}{h^{1/4}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{h}},x_{2}\right)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{1}{(2n)^{1/4}\sqrt{h_{n}}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{2n}h_{n}},x_{2}\right)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}

(C.187)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h_{n}}}\cdot a_{n}\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{h_{n}}\right),

where we defined

(C.188)

a_{n}(x_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{(2n)^{1/4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{2n}},x_{2}\right)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}.

We claim that the functions
a_{n}
have Schwartz seminorms decaying faster than any power of
n
. Indeed, thanks to (
C.188
):

(C.189)

\displaystyle\big\|x_{1}^{j}

\displaystyle\partial_{1}^{k}a_{n}\big\|_{L^{2}}=\left\|x_{1}^{j}\partial_{1}^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A\left(\dfrac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{2n}},x_{2}\right)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(C.190)

\displaystyle\leq(3n)^{1+k}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}x_{1}^{j}\partial_{1}^{k}A(x)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}=(2n)^{1+k}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}x_{1}^{j}\partial_{1}^{k}A(x)\dfrac{(-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})^{\ell}G_{n}(x_{2})}{(2n)^{\ell}}dx_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(C.191)

\displaystyle\leq(3n)^{1+k-\ell}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})^{\ell}x_{1}^{j}\partial_{1}^{k}A(x)G_{n}(x_{2})dx_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq(3n)^{1+k-\ell}\left\|\big(-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\big)^{\ell}x_{1}^{j}\partial_{1}^{k}A\right\|_{L^{2}}.

In the second line we made the substitution
x_{1}\mapsto\sqrt{2n}x_{1}
, then we used that the components of
G_{n}
are eigenvectors of
-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}
with eigenvalues
2n+1,2n-1
. In the third line we used that
-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}
is selfadjoint, then we used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, noting that
|G_{n}|_{L^{2}}=1
. By the Sobolev embedding theorem, (
C.189
) implies that
a_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, with its Schwartz seminorms decaying faster than any power of
n
.

In particular,
{\widehat{a}}_{n}
is admissible of order
-\infty
, see (
C.169
).
Theorem
C.6
produces then a leading-order description of
f_{n}
in terms of
a_{n}
:

(C.192)

f_{n}(t,x_{1})-\mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}}a_{n}(t,x_{1})=O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(h_{n}n^{-\infty}\big)=O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(h^{1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).

To deduce (
C.182
), it suffices to take tensor products of (
C.192
) with
G_{n,h}
and to sum over
n
. Using that the functions
G_{n,h}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

(C.193)

F(t,x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}(t,x_{1})G_{n,h}(x_{2})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}}a_{n}(t,x_{1})G_{n,h}(x_{2})+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(h^{1/2}\big)=\mathfrak{E}A(t,x)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(h^{1/2}\big).

To deduce (
C.183
), we simply apply (
C.174
). This completes the proof. ∎

Proof of Theorem
C.1
.

Theorem
C.1
is now a quick consequence of (
C.182
). We recall Cramer’s inequality for Hermite functions:
|g_{n}|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\pi^{-1/4}
, see e.g.
[
I61
]
; therefore
|G_{n,h}|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2(\pi h)^{-1/4}
. We deduce that

(C.194)

\displaystyle\big|\mathfrak{E}A(t,x)-\mathfrak{E}_{0}a_{0}(t,x_{1})G_{0,h}(x_{2})\big|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}}a_{n}(t,x_{1})G_{n,h}(x_{2})\right|

(C.195)

\displaystyle\leq 2(\pi h)^{-1/4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\big|\mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}}a_{n}(t,x_{1})\big|\leq\dfrac{C}{h^{1/4}\sqrt{t}},

where we eventually used the uniform bound in (
C.140
) for
\mathfrak{E}_{n,h_{n}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

C.6.
The case
\lambda
constant

When
\lambda
is constant, then we can find explicitly the flow (
C.54
):

(C.196)

\begin{gathered}x_{t}=x_{0}+\lambda\dfrac{\xi}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle}t,\qquad\xi_{t}=\xi_{0}.\\
\Rightarrow\quad F(t,x,\xi)=x+\lambda\dfrac{\xi}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle}t,\qquad H(t,x,\xi)=x-\lambda\dfrac{\xi}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle}t.\end{gathered}

Hence the solution to the eikonal equation is explicit, given by

(C.197)

\varphi(t,x,\xi)=x\xi-\lambda\sqrt{1+\xi^{2}}.

It follows that
\varphi
is globally defined and satisfies the estimate (
C.97
) for all
t>0
. Likewise,
\alpha,b_{0},b_{1},b
are defined for all
t>0
(and admissible of order
-\infty
). It follows that the parametrices
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
are defined for all
t>0
and satisfy

(C.198)

\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}a(t,x)=\sum_{\pm}\left(e^{\pm i\varphi/\epsilon}\sqrt{\pm\dfrac{i}{2\pi\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}b^{\pm}_{n,\epsilon}(0,0,\cdot)^{\top}{\widehat{a}}_{n}\right)\big(t,x,\pm\Xi(t,x)\big)+O_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big(\epsilon^{1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).

Therefore, we deduce that Theorem
C.7
holds for all fixed
t>0
.

Remark C.4
.

This is valid for fixed time; we briefly discuss long-time alterations when
\mu
is non-zero. One get sufficiently instruction by looking at the case
\lambda=1,\mu=1
,
s=0
. A calculation shows that the function
\alpha
from (
C.117
) depends on
\xi
:

(C.199)

\alpha(t,x,\xi)=e^{-2nit\left\langle\xi\right\rangle\xi}.

When
t,\xi
are bounded, we do not expect this difference to induce any qualitative change of behavior of the packet. As
t\rightarrow\infty
however, (
C.199
) oscillates rapidly for fixed
\xi
; we therefore expect modified dispersive estimates in long time. We tracked down such effects when studying dynamical edge states in magnetic field, see
[
BBD22
]
: the wavepacket approximation there is valid for
T\sim h^{-1/8}
, smaller than
T\sim h^{-1/2}
for the magnetic free case
[
BB+21
]
.

Chapter D
Fourier reconstruction

We now return to the equation
(hD_{t}+\not{D})\Psi_{t}=0
, where
\not{D}
is no longer the model operator of Chapter
C
but instead is the Weyl quantization of a symbol
{\not{\partial}}{}
satisfying
\bm{(\mathcal{A}_{1}})-\bm{(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
(see §
A.3.1
). We prove Theorem
A.2
: semiclassically near
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}
, waves split in traveling and dispersive parts.

We follow a standard strategy: we reduce the equation
(hD_{t}+\not{D})\Psi_{t}=0
to the canonical model
(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})\Psi_{t}=0
studied in §
C
using the Fourier integral operator
\mathcal{F}
constructed in Theorem
B.2
. We then confirm that the traveling / dispersive waves emerging in §
C.5.3
keep their qualitative features under backward application of
\mathcal{F}
.

D.1.
Preliminary estimates

Our program starts with some preliminary estimates that study how Fourier integral operators of the form (
) act on the quantities emerging in (
C.183
).

D.1.1.
Traveling wave

We study integrals of the form

(D.1)

L(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\xi)-y\xi}{h}}c(x,\xi)\cdot\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}q\left(\dfrac{y}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\dfrac{dyd\xi}{(2\pi h)^{2}},\qquad\text{where:}

•

q
is a Schwartz function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
;

•

c
is a symbol bounded together with all its derivatives;

•

\phi
generates the generating function of the symplectomorphism
\kappa
produced in Lemma
B.6
.

In particular, the equation
\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)=0
has a unique solution, denoted
x_{\star}
below. With
\xi_{\star}=\partial_{x}\phi(x_{\star},0)
, the pair
(x_{\star},\xi_{\star})
is uniquely characterized by the relation

(D.2)

\kappa(0,0)=(x_{\star},\xi_{\star}).

Our next lemma is (a version of) a standard result about action of Fourier integral operators on wavepackets. Essentially, (
D.1
) is a wavepacket concentrated at
(x_{\star},\xi_{\star})
(see Definition
A.1
).

Lemma D.1
.

Under the above assumptions and notations, we have
L(x)=W(x)+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})
, where
W
is a wavepacket concentrated at
(x_{\star},\xi_{\star})
.

Proof.

1. We first realize the integral in
y
and obtain

(D.3)

L(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\xi)}{h}}c(x,\xi)\cdot\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}{\widehat{q}}\left(\dfrac{\xi}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}h}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)\cdot{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

We then write a Taylor expansion of
c
near
\xi=0
:

(D.4)

\begin{gathered}c(x,\xi)=c(x,0)+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\xi_{j}\cdot c_{j}(x,\xi),\qquad c_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{4}).\end{gathered}

Plugging (
D.4
) in (
D.3
), we end up with
L=L_{1}+\sqrt{h}\cdot L_{2}
, where:

(D.5)

\displaystyle L_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{c(x,0)}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}}\cdot{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

(D.6)

\displaystyle L_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}}c_{j}(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)\cdot\xi_{j}{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

We observe that
L_{2}
is the sum of two terms of the form (
D.3
). Each of these term corresponds to a semiclassical Fourier integral operator applied to a function with fixed
L^{2}
-norm. We deduce that
L_{2}=O_{L^{2}}(1)
, uniformly as
h\rightarrow 0
; hence
L=L_{1}+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})
.

2. We now write a Taylor expansion of
\phi
near
\xi=0
:

(D.7)

\begin{gathered}\phi(x,\xi)=\phi(x,0)+\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)\xi+\dfrac{1}{2}\left\langle\xi,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\phi(x,0)\xi\right\rangle+\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\xi_{\alpha}\psi_{\alpha}(x,\xi).\end{gathered}

The functions
\psi_{\alpha}
are smooth, compactly supported because
\phi
is quadratic outside a compact set (see (i) from Lemma
B.6
). As in
[
H90
, §7.7]
, we define for
{\theta}\in[0,1]
:

(D.8)

\begin{gathered}\phi_{\theta}(x,\xi)=\phi(x,0)+\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)\xi+\dfrac{1}{2}\left\langle\xi,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\phi(x,0)\xi\right\rangle+{\theta}\cdot\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\xi_{\alpha}\psi_{\alpha}(x,\xi),\\
L_{\theta}(x)=\dfrac{c(x,0)}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi_{\theta}(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}}\cdot{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}.\end{gathered}

We then have
\phi_{1}=\phi
and therefore:

(D.9)

\displaystyle L_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=L_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{dL_{\theta}(x)}{d{\theta}}d{\theta}

(D.10)

\displaystyle=L_{0}(x)+\sqrt{h}\cdot\dfrac{c(x,0)}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\phi_{\theta}(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}}\psi_{\alpha}(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)\cdot\xi^{\alpha}{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}\right)d{\theta}.

Again, the second term corresponds to a (integral over
{\theta}
of sums of) semiclassical Fourier integral operator applied to a normalized function. In particular, owing to the term
\sqrt{h}
in front of it, we deduce that
L_{1}=L_{0}+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})
.

3. It remains to estimate the expression

(D.11)

L_{0}(x)=\dfrac{c(x,0)e^{\frac{i}{h}\phi(x,0)}}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\xi\frac{\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)}{\sqrt{h}}}\cdot e^{\frac{i}{2}\left\langle\xi,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\phi(x,0)\xi\right\rangle}\cdot{\widehat{q}}(\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

We write the integral in (
D.11
) as

(D.12)

\begin{gathered}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\xi\frac{\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)}{\sqrt{h}}}\cdot\rho(x,\xi)\dfrac{d\xi}{(2\pi)^{2}}=\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\rho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\rai The function
\rho(x,\cdot)
is Schwartz, with seminorms uniformly bounded for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
:
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\phi
is constant where
\phi
is quadratic, hence outside a compact set. Hence so is its inverse Fourier transform. Recall that
x_{\star}
is the solution to
\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)=0
and Taylor-expand
\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)
near
x_{\star}
:

(D.13)

\partial_{\xi}\phi(x,0)=\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)(x-x_{\star})+\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\Psi_{\alpha}(x)(x-x_{\star})^{\alpha},\qquad\Psi_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Using that
\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)
is not singular, we can perform successive Taylor expansions of (
D.12
) near
x_{\star}
. We first obtain
