\displaystyle=\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\rho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}\left(x_{\star},\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)\frac{x-x_{\star}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h}),

We then go back to (
D.11
). We recall that
\xi_{\star}=\partial_{x}\phi(x_{\star},0)
. By replacing
c(x,0)
by
c(x_{\star},0)
and
\phi(x,0)
by its Taylor expansion at order
2
, we deduce that

(D.16)

L_{0}(x)=\dfrac{c(x_{\star},0)e^{\frac{i}{h}\phi(x_{\star},0)+\frac{i}{h}\xi_{\star}(x-x_{\star})}}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot e^{i\left\langle z,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)z\right\rangle}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\rho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}\left(x_{\star},\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)z\right)\Bigg|_{z=\frac{x-x_{\star}}{\sqrt{h}}}+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h}),

Hence,
L_{0}
and
L
, are wavepackets concentrated at
(x_{\star},\xi_{\star})
up to a remainder
O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})
. ∎

D.1.2.
Dispersive wave

Now we fix
n\geq 1
,
h>0
and we prove upper bounds on

(D.17)

B(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\xi)-y\xi}{h}}c(x,\xi)e^{i\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})/\sqrt{h}}q(y_{1})g_{n}\left(\dfrac{y_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\dfrac{dyd\xi}{h^{2}},\qquad\text{where:}

•

q
is admissible of order
-\infty
(it particular it can depend implicitly on
n,h
) and has support in a bounded interval
I
;

•

g_{n}
is a Hermite function, see §
C.2.1
;

•

\vartheta_{n}
satisfies the bound

(D.18)

\big|\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})\big|\leq Cn^{1/2}{\operatorname{d}}(y,\mathbb{R}\setminus I)^{-3j};

•

\phi
generates the generating function of the symplectomorphism
\kappa
produced in Lemma
B.6
.

Lemma D.2
.

There exists
C
independent of
n,h
such that under the above assumptions,

(D.19)

\big|B(x)\big|\leq Cn^{-\infty},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

1. We first prepare the integral
B(x)
. Up to multiplying by a phase, we may assume that
\phi(x,0)=0
. By translating
y_{1}
, we may assume that
\partial_{y_{1}}\phi(x,0)=0
. We now make the substitution
\xi\mapsto\sqrt{h}\xi
and
y_{2}\mapsto\sqrt{h}y_{2}
so that

(D.20)

B(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}+i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}-iy_{2}\xi_{2}}\cdot c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)q(y_{1})g_{n}(y_{2})\dfrac{dyd\xi}{\sqrt{h}}.

We now realize the integral over
y_{2}
and obtain:

(D.21)

B(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}+i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)q(y_{1}){\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\dfrac{dy_{1}d\xi}{\sqrt{h}}.

We comment that since the Fourier transform of the harmonic oscillator is itself,
{\widehat{g}}_{n}
equals
g_{n}
(up to a phase).

2. Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
; in Steps
2-4
we assume that
q
has support in the set
\big\{|\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}-\ell|\leq 1\big\}
. Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, equal to
1
on
[-3,3]
and to
0
outside
[-4,4]
. We write
B(x)=B_{1}(x)+B_{2}(x)
with:

(D.22)

\begin{gathered}B_{1}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}+i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)q(y_{1})\chi(\xi_{1}-\ell){\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\dfrac{dy_{1}d\xi}{\sqrt{h}},\\
B_{2}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}+i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)q(y_{1})\big(1-\chi(\xi_{1}-\ell)\big){\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\dfrac{dy_{1}d\xi}{\sqrt{h}}.\end{gathered}

We focus on the integral in
y_{1}
emerging in
B_{2}(x)
. For
(y_{1},\xi_{1})
in the domain of integration,

(D.23)

\big|\xi_{1}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}(y_{1})\big|\geq|\xi_{1}-\ell|-|\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}(y_{1})-\ell|\geq\dfrac{|\xi_{1}-\ell|}{2}+\dfrac{3}{2}-1\geq\dfrac{\left\langle\xi_{1}-\ell\right\rangle}{2}.

We integrate by parts twice according to the identity

(D.24)

\dfrac{-i\sqrt{h}}{\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}(y_{1})-\xi_{1}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}e^{i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-\xi_{1}y_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}=e^{i\frac{\vartheta_{n}(y_{1})-\xi_{1}y_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}.

This yields

(D.25)

\begin{gathered}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\vartheta(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}q(y_{1})\dfrac{dy_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}\right|=\sqrt{h}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\vartheta(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}\dfrac{1}{\vartheta^{\prime}(y_{1})-\xi_{1}}\right)^{2}q(y_{1})dy_{1}\right|\\
\leq C\sqrt{nh}\sum_{j=0}^{2}\left\langle\xi_{1}-\ell\right\rangle^{j-4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\big|\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q(y_{1})\big|dy_{1}\leq\dfrac{C\sqrt{nh}}{\left\langle\xi_{1}-\ell\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}.\end{gathered}

In the second line we used the bound (
D.18
) on derivatives of
\vartheta
. We note that since
q
is smooth with support in
I
, so is
{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q
. We then bound the quantity
B_{2}(x)
defined in (
D.22
) using (
D.25
) and the integrability of
\left\langle\xi_{1}\right\rangle^{-2}
:

(D.26)

\begin{gathered}\big|B_{2}(x)\big|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(1-\chi(\xi_{1})\big)\big|{\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\big|\cdot\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\vartheta(y_{1})-y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}q(y_{1})\dfrac{dy_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}\right|d\xi\\
\leq C\sqrt{nh}\big\|{\widehat{g}}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{d\xi_{1}}{\left\langle\xi_{1}-\ell\right\rangle^{2}}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\sqrt{nh}\big\|g_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}.\end{gathered}

4. We now bound
B_{1}(x)
:

(D.27)

\big|B_{1}(x)\big|\leq\|q\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|{\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\big|\cdot\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}-i\frac{y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)\chi(\xi_{1}-\ell)d\xi_{1}\right|\dfrac{dy_{1}d\xi_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}.

We focus on the integral over
\xi_{1}
. We Taylor-expand
\phi
in
\xi_{1}
near
0
: there exists a smooth function
\psi
, bounded together with all its derivatives, such that

(D.28)

\phi(x,\xi)=\phi(x,0,\xi_{2})+\partial_{y_{1}}\phi(x,0,\xi_{2})\xi_{1}+\xi_{1}^{2}\psi(x,\xi).

We emphasize that
\psi
is constant outside a compact set because
\phi
is quadratic outside a compact set. From plugging (
D.28
) in (
D.27
), we deduce

(D.29)

\begin{gathered}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\phi(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)}{h}-i\frac{y_{1}\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}}c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)\chi(\xi_{1}-\ell)d\xi_{1}\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{\partial_{y_{1}}\phi(0,\xi_{2})-y_{1}}{\sqrt{h}}\xi_{1}}\cdot f_{h,x,\xi_{2}}(\xi_{1})d\xi_{1}\right|\\
=\left|\widehat{f}_{h,x,\xi_{2}}\left(\dfrac{y_{1}-\partial_{y_{1}}\phi(0,\xi_{2})}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\right|,\qquad f_{h,x,\xi_{2}}(\xi_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ c(x,\sqrt{h}\xi)e^{i\xi_{1}^{2}\psi(\sqrt{h}\xi)}\chi(\xi_{1}-\ell).\end{gathered}

Plugging (
D.29
) in (
D.27
), we obtain

(D.30)

\big|B_{1}(x)\big|\leq\|q\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|{\widehat{g}}_{n}(\xi_{2})\big|\cdot\left|\widehat{f}_{h,\xi_{2}}\left(\dfrac{y_{1}-\partial_{y_{1}}\phi(0,\xi_{2})}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\right|\dfrac{dy_{1}d\xi_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\leq\|q\|_{L^{\infty}}\|{\widehat{g}}_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\cdot\sup_{\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}}\big\|\widehat{f}_{h,\xi_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}}.

We control
\widehat{f}_{h,x,\xi_{2}}
using Holder’s
L^{1}-L^{\infty}
bound and that the Fourier transform is bounded from
L^{1}
to
L^{\infty}
:

(D.31)

\big\|\widehat{f}_{h,x,\xi_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}}=\big\|\left\langle y_{1}\right\rangle^{-2}\left\langle y_{1}\right\rangle^{2}\widehat{f}_{h,x,\xi_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}}\leq\big\|(1+\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{2})f_{h,x,\xi_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\left\langle\ell\right\rangle^{4},

where the constant
C
is independent of
h,x,\xi_{2}
.
We recall that we assumed that the support of
q
is contained in the set
\{|\vartheta^{\prime}-\ell|\leq 1\}
. Therefore,
\ell
and
\vartheta^{\prime}
are comparable on the support of
q
; moreover
\left\langle\vartheta^{\prime}\right\rangle
is controlled by
{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-3}
, see (
D.18
). We deduce that

(D.32)

\big|B_{1}(x)\big|\leq C\|q\|_{L^{\infty}}\|{\widehat{g}}_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\cdot\left\langle\ell\right\rangle^{4}\leq C\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-12}q\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g_{n}\|_{L^{1}}.

We conclude that whenever
{\operatorname{supp}}(q)\subset\{|\vartheta^{\prime}-\ell|\leq 1\}
,

(D.33)

\big|B(x)\big|\leq C\sqrt{n}\|g_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\left(\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-12}q\big\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}\right).

5. We now prove a bound on
\|g_{n}\|_{L^{1}}
. From Cauchy–Schwarz and integrability of
\left\langle x_{2}\right\rangle^{-2}
over
\mathbb{R}
, we have:

(D.34)

\|g_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\|\left\langle x_{2}\right\rangle g_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=\left\langle(1+x_{2}^{2})g_{n},g_{n}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle 1-\partial^{2}_{2}+x_{2}^{2})g_{n},g_{n}\right\rangle=2n+2,

where we used that
g_{n}
is an eigenvector of
-\partial_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}
for the eigenvalue
2n+1
. Hence, (
D.33
) becomes

(D.35)

\big|B(x)\big|\leq Cn\left(\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-12}q\big\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-9}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}\right).

6. To conclude, we must take off the restriction on the support of
q
. Let
{\widetilde{\chi}}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with support in
[-1,1]
such that
\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\chi}}(y_{1}-\ell)=1
. We decompose
q
as

(D.36)

q(y_{1})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\chi}}\big(\vartheta^{\prime}(y_{1})-\ell\big)q(y_{1}).

The terms in the sum satisfy the support condition of Steps 2-4. The additional price to pay is related to the growth of (at most two) derivatives of
{\widetilde{\chi}}\big(\vartheta^{\prime}(y_{1})-\ell\big)
, see (
D.33
). These are controlled by
\vartheta^{\prime\prime 2}+\vartheta^{\prime\prime\prime}
, hence by
n
times
12
additional moments of
{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-1}
. Summing these estimates produces

(D.37)

\big|B(x)\big|\leq Cn^{2}\left(\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-12}q\big\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\big\|{\operatorname{d}}(y_{1},I)^{-21}\partial_{y_{1}}^{j}q\big\|_{L^{1}}\right).

We can then bound these moments by sufficiently many derivatives of
q
, and use that
q
is admissible of order
-\infty
to conclude.
∎

D.1.3.
Pseudodifferential action

We recall that
g_{n,h}
are semiclassical Hermite functions (see §
) and
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
are the block parametrices defined in (
C.181
).

Lemma D.3
.

Let
a
be an admissible function of order
-\infty
; let
r=\mathcal{O}(3)+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)+O(h^{2})
. Then uniformly in
t\in(0,T)
,
h\in(0,1)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(D.38)

r^{w}\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)=O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{3/2}n^{-\infty}t^{-1/2}\big).

Proof.

1. First assume that
r=O(h^{2})
. Then there is nothing to prove, because
r^{w}
has
L^{2}
-norm controlled by
h^{2}
and the
L^{2}
-norm of
\mathfrak{E}_{n}a
is controlled by
n^{-\infty}t^{-1/2}
, see (
C.171
).

2. We now assume that
r=O(h)\mathcal{O}(1)
. Then we can write

(D.39)

\begin{gathered}r=h(x_{2}r_{1}+\xi_{1}r_{2}+\xi_{2}r_{3})=h(x_{2}\#r_{1}+\xi_{1}\#r_{2}+\xi_{2}\#r_{3})+O(h^{2}),\\
\qquad r^{w}=h(r_{1}^{w}\cdot hD_{1}+r_{2}^{w}\cdot x_{2}+r_{3}^{w}\cdot hD_{2})+O(h^{2})^{w}.\end{gathered}

Above,
\#
denotes the operation induced at the level of symbols by the composition of two pseudodifferential operators.
Again, for the purpose of proving estimates of the form (
D.38
), we can ignore the term
O(h^{2})^{w}
in (
D.39
). Moreover, from
2x_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{a^{*}}
and
2\partial_{2}=\mathfrak{a}^{*}-\mathfrak{a^{*}}
, we have the relations

(D.40)

\begin{gathered}2x_{2}g_{n,h}=h^{1/2}(\sqrt{2n+2}g_{n+1,h}+\sqrt{2n}g_{n-1,h}),\\
2h\partial_{2}g_{n,h}=h^{1/2}(\sqrt{2n}g_{n-1,h}+\sqrt{2n+2}g_{n+1,h}),\end{gathered}

where we recall that
g_{-1}=0
by convention.
Therefore, using that
r_{2}^{w}
is bounded on
L^{2}
, independently of
h
, and the bound (
C.171
) for the
L^{2}
-norm of
\mathfrak{E}_{n}a
when
n\geq 1
:

(D.41)

\big\|r_{2}^{w}\cdot x_{2}\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(nh)^{1/2}\sum_{\pm}\big\|\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n\pm 1}\big\|_{L^{2}}=O(h^{1/2}t^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}).

This is true also when
n=0
, as can be directly checked from the formula (
C.181
) for
\mathfrak{E}_{0}
. Likewise, the same approach yields

(D.42)

\big\|r_{3}^{w}\cdot(hD_{2})\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(nh)^{1/2}\sum_{\pm}\big\|\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n\pm 1}\big\|_{L^{2}}=O(h^{1/2}t^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}).

We now focus on the term
r_{1}^{w}\cdot hD_{1}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}
. If
n\geq 1
, using that
r_{1}^{w}
is bounded on
L^{2}
:

(D.43)

\big\|r_{1}^{w}\cdot\big(hD_{1}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\big\|hD_{1}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\big\|_{L^{2}}.

We then use the integral formula (
C.170
) for
\mathfrak{E}_{n}a
. It gives:

(D.44)

\displaystyle hD_{1}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a

\displaystyle=(2nh)^{1/2}\cdot\sum_{\pm}\dfrac{\pm 1}{2\pi h_{n}^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})e^{\pm i\varphi(t,x_{1},\pm\xi_{1})/h_{n}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(0,0,\xi_{1})^{\top}{\widehat{a}}(\xi_{1})d\xi

(D.45)

\displaystyle+h\cdot\sum_{\pm}\dfrac{1}{2\pi h_{n}^{1/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\pm i\varphi(t,x_{1},\pm\xi_{1})/h_{n}}\cdot D_{x_{1}}b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(0,0,\xi_{1})^{\top}{\widehat{a}}(\xi_{1})d\xi.

The above integrals are of the form (
C.137
). Since they come with coefficients
h^{1/2}
and
h
, we get

(D.46)

hD_{1}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a=O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/2}t^{-1/2}n^{-\infty}\big).

This remains true when
n=0
, as can be directly verified from the formula (
C.181
). Plugging (
D.46
) in (
D.43
), and going back to (
D.39
), we conclude that (
D.38
) holds whenever
r=O(h)\mathcal{O}(1)
.

3. We now assume that
r=\mathcal{O}(3)
. Then we can write

(D.47)

\begin{gathered}r=\sum_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=3}x_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}\xi_{1}^{\alpha_{2}}\xi_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}r_{\alpha},\\
r^{w}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=3}r_{\alpha}^{w}\cdot x_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(hD_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}(hD_{2})^{\alpha_{3}}+\big(\mathcal{O}(2)O(h)\big)^{w}+O(h^{2})^{w},\end{gathered}

where we used the expansion for the symbol of the composition of two pseudodifferential operators; see e.g.
[
Z12
, Theorem 4.12]
. Again, we can ignore the term
O(h^{2})^{w}
. Symbols
\mathcal{O}(2)O(h)
are a fortiori
\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)
, so therefore we can deal with them using Step 2. We now focus on the first term. Using the relations (
D.40
), we can write

(D.48)

x_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(hD_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}(hD_{2})^{\alpha_{3}}\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)=\sum_{\alpha_{2}=0}^{3}(hD_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\cdot h^{\frac{3-\alpha_{2}}{2}}\sum_{j=\alpha_{2}-3}^{3-\alpha_{2}}c_{n,\alpha_{2},j}g_{n\pm j,h},

for some coefficients
c_{n,\alpha_{2},j}
that are
O(n^{3/2})
. Following the same path as that leading to (
D.46
), we conclude that

(D.49)

\big\|r_{\alpha}^{w}\cdot x_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(hD_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}(hD_{2})^{\alpha_{3}}\cdot\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\otimes g_{n,h}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq Cn^{3/2}\sum_{\alpha_{2}=0}^{3}h^{\frac{3-\alpha_{2}}{2}}\big\|(hD_{1})^{\alpha_{2}}\mathfrak{E}_{n}a\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ch^{3/2}n^{-\infty}t^{-1/2}.

This completes the proof. ∎

D.2.
Proof of Theorem A.2

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

1. Fix
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Gamma
. According to Theorem
B.2
, there exists:

•

An operator
\mathcal{F}
of the form (
B.11
);

•

Three smooth functions
\lambda:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mu,s:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
;

•

A pseudodifferential operator
R
with symbol
\mathcal{O}(3)+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
near
0
;

such that, with
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
of the form (
B.12
):

(D.50)

\not{D}\mathcal{F}\ =\mathcal{F}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}+R).

2. Let
\psi_{0}
be a wavepacket concentrated at
(x_{0},\xi_{0})
, see Definition
A.1
. Up to a phase
e^{is_{h}}
,

(D.51)

\psi_{0}(x)=\dfrac{e^{\frac{i}{h}\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})}}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot A\left(\dfrac{x-x_{0}}{\sqrt{h}}\right),\qquad A\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We define
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then
{\widetilde{A}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
through the implicit relations:

(D.52)

\begin{gathered}c(x_{0},0)e^{\frac{i}{h}\phi(x_{0},0)}\cdot e^{i\left\langle y,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)y\right\rangle}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\rho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}_{0}\left(\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)y\right)=A(y),\\
\rho_{0}(\xi)=e^{\frac{i}{2}\left\langle\xi,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{0},0)\xi\right\rangle}\widehat{{\widetilde{A}}}(\xi),\qquad{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{h}}{\widetilde{A}}\left(\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{h}}\right).\end{gathered}

Then, noting that
\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}
is an integral of the form (
D.1
), and following Step 3 in the proof of Lemma
D.1
, we obtain

(D.53)

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}(x)=\dfrac{c(x_{0},0)\cdot e^{\frac{i}{h}\phi(x_{0},0)+\frac{i}{h}\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})}}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot e^{\frac{i}{h}\left\langle z,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(x_{0},0)z\right\rangle}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\rho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}_{0}\left(\partial_{x\xi}^{2}\phi(x_{\star},0)z\right)\Bigg|_{z=\frac{x-x_{0}}{\sqrt{h}}}+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})\\
=\dfrac{e^{\frac{i}{h}\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})}}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot A\left(\dfrac{x-x_{0}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h})=\psi_{0}(x)+O_{L^{2}}(\sqrt{h}).\end{gathered}

3. We now define
{\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}(x)=(\mathfrak{E}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(t,x)
. Thanks to (
D.50
), we have:

(D.54)

(hD_{t}+\not{D})\big(\psi_{t}-\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}\big)=-\mathcal{F}(hD_{t}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}+R){\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}.

We now apply Duhamel’s formula as in
[
BB+21
, Lemma 3.5]
:

(D.55)

\big\|\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}-\psi_{t}\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\big\|\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}-\psi_{0}\big\|_{L^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{t}\big\|\mathcal{F}(hD_{\tau}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}+R){\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}\big\|_{L^{2}}d\tau.

To study the right-hand-side, we decompose
{\widetilde{A}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n}
according to (
C.181
). Therefore, using that
\psi_{\tau}=\mathfrak{E}{\widetilde{A}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}
and the decomposition (
C.15
) of
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
, we deduce that

(D.56)

(hD_{\tau}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}){\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}=(hD_{\tau}+L)\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{2nh}(h_{n}D_{\tau}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}.

In particular, thanks to (
C.28
) and (
C.178
):

(D.57)

\displaystyle\big\|(hD_{\tau}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}){\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|(hD_{\tau}+L)\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{2nh}\big\|(h_{n}D_{\tau}+\not{\mathfrak{D}})\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{L^{2}}

(D.58)

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{3/2}+Ch^{1/2}\tau^{-1/2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}^{2}n^{1/2-\infty}=O(h^{3/2}\tau^{-1/2}).

Likewise, thanks to Lemma
D.3
:

(D.59)

\big\|R{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big\|R\big(\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}=O(\tau^{-1/2}h^{3/2}).

As a semiclassical Fourier integral operator,
\mathcal{F}
is bounded (independently of
h
) on
L^{2}
. Therefore, plugging the bounds (
D.58
) and (
D.59
) in (
D.55
) and using that
\tau^{-1/2}
is integrable near
0
, we conclude that uniformly in
t\in(0,T)
and
h>0
:

(D.60)

\psi_{t}=\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}+O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/2}).

3. According to (
D.60
), the solution
\psi_{t}
to
(hD_{t}+\not{D})\psi_{t}=0
and the function
\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{E}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}
nearly coincide. To complete the proof of Theorem
A.2
, it remains to understand
\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{E}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}
. We have:

(D.61)

\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{E}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}=\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}+\mathcal{F}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}.

We recall that
\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}
is a wavepacket concentrated at
(x_{t}^{+},0)
, where
x_{t}^{+}=e^{t\lambda(x_{1})\partial_{x_{1}}}(0)
, see (
C.6
). Therefore, according to Lemma
D.1
,
\mathcal{F}\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}
is a wavepacket concentrated at
(x_{t},\xi_{t})=\kappa(x_{t}^{+},0)
. Because of Lemma
B.7
, this point satisfies the ODE

(D.62)

\dfrac{d(x_{t},\xi_{t})}{dt}=d\kappa(x_{t},\xi_{t})\cdot(\dot{x_{t}^{+}},0)=d\kappa(x_{t},\xi_{t})\cdot\lambda(x_{t}^{+})\partial_{x_{1}}=V_{\not{\partial}}{}(x_{t},\xi_{t}).

That is,
\mathfrak{E}_{0}{\widetilde{a}}_{0}\otimes G_{0,h}(t,\cdot)
is concentrated along the integral curve of
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
starting at
(x_{0},\xi_{0})
.

We now focus on the second term in (
D.61
). Fix
t\in(0,T)
and recall that
h_{n}=\sqrt{h/2n}
. Thanks to
C.174
, we have, uniformly in
h\in(0,1),n\geq 1
:

(D.63)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}(t,y_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\left(e^{\pm i\varphi/h_{n}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\pm i}{2\pi\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(0,0,\cdot)^{\top}\widehat{{\widetilde{a}}}_{n}\right)\big(t,y_{1},\pm\Xi(t,y_{1})\big)\cdot G_{n,h}(y_{2})

(D.64)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/4}n^{-\infty}\big)

(D.65)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{h^{1/4}}\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm i\vartheta_{n,\pm}(y_{1})/\sqrt{h}}q_{\pm}(y_{1})\cdot G_{n}\left(\dfrac{y_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)+O_{L^{2}}\big(h^{1/4}n^{-\infty}\big),

where we defined (with intentionally omitted time-dependence):

(D.66)

\begin{gathered}q_{\pm}(y_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \left(\sqrt{\dfrac{\pm i}{2\pi\partial_{\xi}^{2}\varphi}}\cdot b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}b^{\pm}_{n,h_{n}}(0,0,\cdot)^{\top}\widehat{{\widetilde{a}}}_{n}\right)\big(t,y_{1},\pm\Xi(t,y_{1})\big),\\
\vartheta_{n,\pm}(y_{1})\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \sqrt{2n}\cdot\varphi\big(t,y_{1},\pm\Xi(t,y_{1})\big).\end{gathered}

Fourier integral operators are bounded on
L^{2}
, hence we can forget the remainder term in (
D.64
). The function
q
is smooth, admissible of order
-\infty
, supported in
I_{t}
– see the discussion after the proof of Corollary
C.3
. Moreover the function
\vartheta_{n,\pm}
satisfies the estimates (
D.18
), see Lemma
C.6
. We can then apply Lemma
D.2
and obtain that

(D.67)

\begin{gathered}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm i\vartheta_{n,\pm}(y_{1})/\sqrt{h}}q_{\pm}(y_{1})\cdot G_{n}\left(\dfrac{y_{2}}{\sqrt{h}}\right)\right)(x)\right|\leq Cn^{-\infty}.\end{gathered}

Summing over
n
, we conclude from (
D.64
) that

(D.68)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{F}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{E}_{n}{\widetilde{a}}_{n}\otimes G_{n,h}(t,x)\right|\leq Ch^{-1/4}.

This shows that the second term in (
D.61
) is
O_{L^{\infty}}(h^{-1/4})+O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/4})
for any fixed
t\in(0,T)
, hence completing the proof of (
A.26
).

4. To complete the proof of Theorem
A.2
it remains to discuss the case
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
constant and
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{},\pi(\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{})
homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Fix
t>0
and set
L=\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}\cdot t
.

According to Remark
B.3
, we can construct a Fourier integral operator such that (
B.14
) holds with a remainder
R
whose symbol is
\mathcal{O}(3)+\mathcal{O}(1)O(h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
on a neighborhood of
(-L-1,L+1)\times\{0\}^{3}
. Moreover, the estimate (
C.183
) holds for all
t>0
; in particular,
{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}
js (up to
O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/4})
remainder) concentrated in
I_{t}\times\{0\}=(-L,L)\times\{0\}
. As in Step 2, we deduce that
R{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\tau}=O_{L^{2}}(\tau^{-1/2}h^{3/2})
. The Duhamel approach written above implies that
\psi_{t}=\mathcal{F}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{t}+O_{L^{2}}(h^{1/2})
.
The rest of the argument applies with no change. This completes the proof.
∎

Chapter E
Applications to topological insulators

In this section we apply our microlocal approach to fundamental models in the field of topological insulators. The simplest one is the domain wall Dirac operator: we find the speed and orientation of propagating modes. The second example analyzes the effect of an external magnetic field. The third model is a natural extension of the latter in curved geometry. In the fourth application, we consider distortions of standard tight-binding models. By comparing our prediction with the second and third examples, we show that the propagation of modes in distorted lattice can be described in terms of pseudo metric and magnetic field.

E.1.
Domain wall Dirac operator

We first consider the domain wall Dirac operator from §
A.2
:

(E.1)

\not{D}=hD_{1}\sigma_{1}+hD_{2}\sigma_{2}+m(x)\sigma_{3},\qquad{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)=\xi_{1}\sigma_{1}+\xi_{2}\sigma_{2}+m(x)\sigma_{3},

where
m\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
is a smooth function that vanishes transversely along its zero set. Such operators emerge as effective Hamiltonians in graphene; see
[
FLW16
,
LWZ19
,
D19
,
DW20
]
for rigorous derivations starting from continuous, translation-invariant systems.

In this section, we justify that propagating modes for (
E.1
) travel along
m^{-1}(0)
at speed one; and that the estimate (
A.26
) holds for all
t>0
when
m^{-1}(0)
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. This extends some results from
[
BB+21
]
.

E.1.1.
Relevant quantities

We first compute the quantities
M_{\not{\partial}}{}
,
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
,
H_{\not{\partial}}{}
and
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
:

(E.2)

\displaystyle M_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2i}\{{\not{\partial}}{},{\not{\partial}}{}\}(x,\xi)=\partial_{2}m(x)\sigma_{1}-\partial_{1}m(x)\sigma_{2},

(E.3)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{2}m(x)^{2}+\partial_{1}m(x)^{2}\right)^{1/4}=\big\|\nabla m(x)\big\|^{1/2},

(E.4)

\displaystyle H_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\sigma_{2}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+\sigma_{3}\left(\partial_{1}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}+\partial_{2}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}\right)

(E.5)

\displaystyle V_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{{\operatorname{Tr\ }}(M_{\not{\partial}}{}H_{\not{\partial}}{})}{2\lambda^{2}_{\not{\partial}}{}}=\dfrac{1}{\|\nabla m(x)\|}\left(\partial_{1}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}-\partial_{2}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\right)=\dfrac{\nabla m(x)^{\perp}}{\|\nabla m(x)\|},

where
u^{\perp}
denotes the
\pi/2
counterclockwise rotation of a vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We note that
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
is a unit vector tangent to
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}=m^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}^{2}
. In particular, Theorem
A.2
predicts the existence of a coherent state traveling (in physical space) along
\Gamma_{\not{\partial}}{}=m^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}^{2}
at speed one, in the direction pointing to the left of the positive-topology region (see Figure
A.2
). Moreover, its orientation is given by the negative-energy eigenvector of
M_{\not{\partial}}{}
: we find
[-1,e^{i{\theta}(x)}]
where
{\theta}(x)
is the angle between
\nabla m(x)
and the
x_{2}
axis.

E.1.2.
Symplectomorphism

We construct explicitly the symplectomorphism of Theorem
B.1
(and the associated Fourier integral operator), when
{\not{\partial}}{}
is the symbol (
E.1
),
m^{-1}(0)
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
and
\|\nabla m(x)\|=1
is constant along
m^{-1}(0)
. We start with the quadratic form

(E.6)

q(x,\xi)=-\det{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)=\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}+m(x)^{2}.

Introduce

(E.7)

\tau(x)=\nabla m(x)^{\perp},\qquad n(x)=\nabla m(x),\qquad\Omega(x)=\big[\tau(x),n(x)\big].

We note that
n
and
\tau
are normal and tangent to level sets of
m
, in particular to
m^{-1}(0)
; and that
\Omega(x)
is an orthogonal matrix for every
x
. Fix
y_{0}\in m^{-1}(0)
and define
\Phi(x)=e^{x_{2}n}\circ e^{x_{1}\tau}(y_{0})
. Since
m^{-1}(0)
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
,
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism from a tubular neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
to a tubular neighborhood of
m^{-1}(0)
; we extended to a diffeomorhphism on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
; We note that

(E.8)

\dfrac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x_{2}}(x)=n\circ\Phi(x),\qquad\dfrac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x_{1}}(x_{1},0)=\tau\circ\Phi(x_{1},0),\qquad\dfrac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x_{1}}(x)=\tau\circ\Phi(x)+x_{2}u(x),

for some
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
. From (
E.7
) as well as
\tau\cdot\nabla m=0
,
n\cdot\nabla m=1
, we deduce that

(E.9)

\begin{gathered}m\circ\phi(x)=m\circ\phi(x_{1},0)+\int_{0}^{x_{2}}\dfrac{\partial m\circ\Phi(x_{1},y)}{\partial x_{2}}dy=\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\dfrac{\partial m\circ\Phi(y,0)}{\partial x_{1}}dy+\int_{0}^{x_{2}}\dfrac{\partial m\circ\Phi(x_{1},y)}{\partial x_{2}}dy\\
=\int_{0}^{x_{1}}(\tau\cdot\nabla m)\circ\Phi(x_{1},0)dy+\int_{0}^{x_{2}}(n\cdot\nabla m)\circ\Phi(x_{1},y)dy=x_{2}+O(x_{2}^{2}).\end{gathered}

Likewise, from (
E.7
) we obtain

(E.10)

(\nabla\Phi^{\top})^{-1}=\left(\Omega\circ\Phi^{\top}+x_{2}[u,0]^{\top}\right)^{-1}=\Omega\circ\Phi\left({\operatorname{Id}}-x_{2}[u,0]^{\top}\Omega\circ\Phi\right)+O(x_{2}^{2}).

Define now

(E.11)

\kappa(x)=\big(\Phi(x),(\nabla\Phi(x)^{\top})^{-1}\xi\big).

This is a symplectomorphism. Using (
E.6
), (
E.10
) and (
E.9
), we note that

(E.12)

\begin{gathered}q\circ\kappa(x)=\big\|(\nabla\Phi(x)^{\top})^{-1}\xi\big\|^{2}+m\circ\Phi(x)^{2}\\
=\big\|\left({\operatorname{Id}}-x_{2}[u(x),0]^{\top}\Omega(x)\right)\xi\big\|^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+O(x_{2}^{2}\xi^{2},x_{2}\xi_{1}^{2},x_{2}\xi_{2}^{2},x_{2}^{3})\\
=\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}-2x_{2}\xi_{1}\left\langle u(x),\Omega(x)\xi\right\rangle+O(x_{2}^{2}\xi^{2},x_{2}\xi_{1}^{2},x_{2}\xi_{2}^{2},x_{2}^{3}).\end{gathered}

In particular, there are no cubic remainders in
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}^{2}
nor
\xi_{1}x_{2}^{2}
. According to Remark
B.2
,
\mu=0
. Therefore, the coefficient
\nu_{t}
defined in (
C.7
) vanishes. The dynamical edge state associated to (
E.1
) does not experience the weak dispersion discussed below Theorem
C.1
.

E.1.3.
Dispersive estimate

Another feature of the symplectomorphism
\kappa
is that the induced Fourier integral operator is, at least locally, a simple change of coordinates:

(E.13)

\mathcal{F}u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\frac{i}{h}(\Phi(x)-y)\xi}u(y)\dfrac{dy}{(2\pi h)^{2}}=u\circ\Phi(x).

Hence, if we assume that
\|\nabla\kappa(x)\|
is constant along
m^{-1}(0)
then we are in the setup of §
C.6
. The dispersive estimate (
C.9
) holds for all
t>0
, see Corollary
C.2
. Since
L^{\infty}
is invariant under change of coordinates, it transfers back to an estimate on
\not{D}
. This yields Theorem
A.1
(iii).

E.2.
Magnetic Dirac operators

We couple here the operator (
E.1
) with a magnetic field
B=\partial_{1}A_{1}-\partial_{2}A_{1}
,
A\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(E.14)

\displaystyle\not{D}

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}m(x)&hD_{1}-A_{1}(x)-i(hD_{2}-A_{2}(x))\\
hD_{1}-A_{1}(x)+i(hD_{2}-A_{2}(x))&-m(x)\end{matrix}\right]

(E.15)

\displaystyle=\big(hD_{1}-A_{1}(x)\big)\sigma_{1}+(hD_{2}-A_{2}(x)\big)\sigma_{2}+m(x)\sigma_{3},

(E.16)

\displaystyle{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\big(\xi_{1}-A_{1}(x)\big)\sigma_{1}+(\xi_{2}-A_{2}(x)\big)\sigma_{2}+m(x)\sigma_{3}.

As in §
E.1
, we first compute the quantities
M_{\not{\partial}}{}
,
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
,
H_{\not{\partial}}{}
and
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
:

(E.17)

\displaystyle M_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}m(x)\sigma_{1}-\partial_{1}m(x)\sigma_{2}-B(x)\sigma_{3}=\left[\begin{matrix}B(x)&\partial_{2}m(x)+i\partial_{1}m(x)\\
\partial_{2}m(x)-i\partial_{1}m(x)&-B(x)\end{matrix}\right],

(E.18)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\big\|\nabla m(x)\big\|^{2}+B(x)^{2}\right)^{1/4},

(E.19)

\displaystyle H_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\sigma_{2}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+\sigma_{3}\left(\partial_{1}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}+\partial_{2}m(x)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}\right)+O(\partial_{\xi}),

(E.20)

\displaystyle V_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial_{1}m(x)\partial_{x_{1}}-\partial_{2}m(x)\partial_{x_{2}}}{\sqrt{\|\nabla m(x)\big\|2+B(x)^{2}}}+O(\partial_{\xi})=\dfrac{\nabla m(x)^{\perp}}{\sqrt{\|\nabla m(x)\big\|2+B(x)^{2}}}+O(\partial_{\xi}),

where
O(\partial_{\xi})
denotes a vector field generated by
\partial_{\xi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\xi_{2}}
.

We deduce from (
E.20
) the value of the (physical) speed of the propagating mode:

(E.21)

\dfrac{\|\nabla m(x)\big\|}{\sqrt{\|\nabla m(x)\|^{2}+B(x)^{2}}},\qquad x\in m^{-1}(0).

In particular, (
E.21
) is always smaller than
1
: magnetic fields introduced in (
E.1
) through minimal coupling may not speed up the propagation of energy. We also obtain the orientation of the propagating mode: it is the negative-energy eigenvector of (
E.20
),

(E.22)

\left[\begin{matrix}-\cos({\varphi}(x)/2)\\
\sin({\varphi}(x)/2)e^{i{\theta}(x)}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad{\theta}(x)=\arg\big(\partial_{2}m(x)-i\partial_{1}m(x)\big),\qquad{\varphi}(x)=\arg\big(B(x)+i\big\|\nabla m(x)\big\|\big).

Obtaining these expressions by hand in
[
BBD22
]
was a fairly technical procedure, which is now streamlined thanks to our microlocal framework.

E.3.
Dirac operators on surfaces

In this section, we consider Dirac operators
\not{D}
on a curved surface
M
. We find that edge states travel at unit speed (measured with respect to the Riemannian metric). Hence, the curvature does not affect the speed of propagation. We then investigate coupling effect with a magnetic field and derive a formula similar to (
E.21
).

E.3.1.
Definitions

Let
M
be a Riemannian surface. Topological considerations, see e.g.
[
H56
]
, imply that
M
admits a spin structure. In the present context, this means that there exists a complex vector bundle
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow M
of rank
2
, such that
T^{*}M\otimes\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow M
admits a smooth section
\sigma
with

(E.23)

\sigma(x)\xi\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\prime}+\sigma(x)\xi^{\prime}\cdot\sigma(x)\xi=2\left\langle\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle_{g(x)}\cdot{\operatorname{Id}}_{\mathcal{E}_{x}},\qquad x\in M,\quad\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in T_{x}^{*}M,

where
\sigma(x)\xi
denotes the endomorphism
\sigma(x)(\xi,\cdot)
of
\mathcal{E}_{x}
.

Given
x\in M
and
v\in T_{x}
, we let
v^{\perp_{g}}\in T_{x}M
be the unique vector orthogonal to
v
, with same norm, such that
d\operatorname{Vol}(v,v^{\perp_{g}})>0
, where
d\operatorname{Vol}
is the Riemannian volume form (in particular, for
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
\nabla_{g}f^{\perp_{g}}
is always tangent to the level sets of
f
). Using the isomorphism between
T_{x}M
and
T_{x}^{*}M
, we can likewise define
\xi^{\perp_{g}}\in T_{x}^{*}M
for
\xi\in T^{*}_{x}M
. We now set

(E.24)

\sigma_{3}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \dfrac{1}{i}\sigma(x)\xi\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}},\qquad|\xi|_{g(x)}^{2}=1.

We note that the right-hand-side does not depend on
\xi
: indeed, if
\xi^{\prime}\in T_{x}M
has unit norm, then decomposing
\xi^{\prime}=\cos{\theta}\xi+\sin{\theta}\xi^{\perp_{g}}
we obtain

(E.25)

\begin{gathered}\sigma(x)\xi^{\prime}\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\prime\perp_{g}}=\sigma(x)\left(\cos{\theta}\xi+\sin{\theta}\xi^{\perp_{g}}\right)\cdot\sigma(x)\left(\cos{\theta}\xi^{\perp_{g}}-\sin{\theta}\xi\right)\\
=(\cos^{2}{\theta}+\sin^{2}{\theta})\sigma(x)\xi\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}}+\sin{\theta}\cos{\theta}\left((\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}})^{2}-(\sigma(x)\xi)^{2}\right)=\sigma(x)\xi\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}}.\end{gathered}

We used that
\sigma(x)\xi
and
\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}}
anticommute, see (
E.23
); and that
\xi,\xi^{\perp_{g}}
have the same norm.

Given a function
m\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
, we define

(E.26)

\partial(x,\xi)=\sigma(x)\xi+m(x)\sigma_{3}(x),

which is a smooth section of
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow T^{*}M
. Let
\not{D}
be a selfadjoint operator on acting on smooth sections of
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow M
, with principal symbol
{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)
.

E.3.2.
Speed of propagating state

We compute the quantities
\{{\not{\partial}}{},{\not{\partial}}{}\}
and
H_{\not{\partial}}{}
. Let
(x_{1},x_{2})
be normal coordinates centered at a point of
m^{-1}(0)
;
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
be induced local coordinates on the fibers of
T^{*}M
; and
(e_{1},e_{2})
be a local frame on
\mathcal{E}
. Noting that
\sigma(x)\xi
is Hermitian and traceless, we may write (in these coordinates):

(E.27)

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{1}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \sigma(x)(1,0)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}h_{1k}(x)\sigma_{k},\qquad\sigma_{2}(x)\ \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\ \sigma(x)(0,1)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}h_{2k}(x)\sigma_{k},\end{gathered}

for some smooth functions
h_{jk}
defined near
0
. From (
E.23
) and using that we are working in normal coordinates,

(E.28)

\begin{gathered}\sum_{k=1}^{3}h_{jk}(x)h_{\ell k}(x)=g^{j\ell}(x)=\delta_{j\ell}+O(x^{2}).\end{gathered}

Hence,
h_{1}(0)
and
h_{2}(0)
, seen as vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, are orthonormal . Moreover, from (
E.24
) we have

(E.29)

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{3}(x)=\dfrac{\sigma_{1}(x)\sigma_{2}(x)}{i|g^{11}(x)g^{22}(x)|^{1/2}}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{3}h_{2k}(x)h_{1\ell}(x)\dfrac{\sigma_{k}\sigma_{\ell}}{i}+O(x^{2})\\
=\sum_{k,\ell,m=1}^{3}{\varepsilon}_{k\ell m}h_{2k}(0)h_{1\ell}(0)\sigma_{m}+O(x^{2})=\sum_{m=1}^{3}\big(h_{1}(0)\times h_{2}(0)\big)_{m}\sigma_{m}+O(x^{2}).\end{gathered}

Therefore, in the Pauli basis
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
, the matrices
\sigma_{1}(0),\sigma_{2}(0),\sigma_{3}(0)
have coordinates
h_{1}(0)
,
h_{2}(0)
and
h_{1}(0)\times h_{2}(0)
; since
h_{1}(0)
and
h_{2}(0)
are orthonormal,
\sigma_{1}(0),\sigma_{2}(0),\sigma_{3}(0)
form a direct orthonormal basis of traceless, Hermitian
2\times 2
matrices: their matrix in the Pauli basis is
[h_{1},h_{2},h_{1}\times h_{2}]
. After conjugating
{\not{\partial}}{}
with an adequate matrix in
SU(2)
(which does not affect the value of
\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}
or
V_{\not{\partial}}{}
), we can assume that
\sigma_{j}(0)=\sigma_{j}
. It follows that (in these coordinates, and up to conjugation):

(E.30)

\displaystyle{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}\xi_{2}+\big(\partial_{1}m(0)x_{1}+\partial_{2}m(0)x_{2}\big)\sigma_{3}+O(x,\xi)^{2},

(E.31)

\displaystyle M_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}m(0)\sigma_{1}-\partial_{1}m(0)\sigma_{2},

(E.32)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)^{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}m(0)^{2}+\partial_{1}m(0)^{2}=\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\|_{g(0)}^{2},

(E.33)

\displaystyle H_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\sigma_{2}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}-\left(\partial_{1}m(0)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}+\partial_{2}m(0)\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}\right)\sigma_{3},

(E.34)

\displaystyle V_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial_{1}m(0)\partial_{x_{2}}-\partial_{2}m(0)\partial_{x_{1}}}{\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\|_{g(0)}}=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}m(0)^{\perp_{g}}}{\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\|_{g(0)}},

where we used that
g(0)=dx_{1}^{2}+dx_{2}^{2}
in normal coordinates centered at
0
. Going back to coordinate-free expression, and recalling that the origin of the normal coordinates system was an arbitrary point in
m^{-1}(0)
, we conclude that on
\Gamma=m^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}^{2}
:

(E.35)

V_{\not{\partial}}{}(x,0)=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}m(x)^{\perp_{g}}}{\|\nabla_{g}m(x)\|_{g}},\qquad x\in m^{-1}(0).

Remark E.1
.

The identity (
E.35
) implies that even in curved geometry, the speed of dynamical edge states for Dirac operators defined by semiclassical quantizations of (
E.26
) (measured with respect to the metric
g
) is independent of the choice of interface function
m
.

We believe that a non-vanishing curvature may lead to a coefficient
\nu_{t}\neq 0
in (
C.7
), in particular to dispersion of edge states as
t\rightarrow\infty
; we do not investigate this effect further.

E.3.3.
Coupling to magnetic fields

Turning on a magnetic
B\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
in (
E.26
) amounts to translating the momentum
\xi
by a smooth one-form
A
on
M
such that
dA=Bd\operatorname{Vol}_{g}
; see e.g.
[
BR20
]
for the Schrödinger operator. This produces the symbol

(E.36)

\partial(x,\xi)=\sigma(x)\big(\xi-A(x)\big)+m(x)\sigma_{3}(x).

To compute the speed of a propagating state in curved geometry, with the magnetic field
B
, it suffices again to fix
x\in M
and work in normal coordinates. Write (in this system of coordinates)
A(x)=A_{1}(x)dx_{1}+A_{2}(x)dx_{2}
and note that
d\operatorname{Vol}_{g}(0)=dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}
; hence

(E.37)

B(0)=\partial_{1}A_{2}(0)-\partial_{2}A_{1}(0).

In particular, (
E.32
) becomes:

(E.38)

\displaystyle{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\big(\xi_{j}-A_{1}(0)-\partial_{x_{1}}A_{j}(0)x_{1}-\partial_{x_{2}}A_{j}(0)x_{2}\big)\sigma_{j}+\big(\partial_{1}m(0)x_{1}+\partial_{2}m(0)x_{2}\big)\sigma_{3}+O(x,\xi)^{2},

(E.39)

\displaystyle M_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}m(0)\sigma_{1}-\partial_{1}m(0)\sigma_{2}+\big(\partial_{1}A_{2}(0)-\partial_{2}A_{1}(0)\big)\sigma_{3}=\partial_{2}m(0)\sigma_{1}-\partial_{1}m(0)\sigma_{2}+B(0)\sigma_{3},

(E.40)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)^{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}m(0)^{2}+\partial_{1}m(0)^{2}+B(0)^{2}=\big\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\big\|_{g}^{2}+B(0)^{2},

(E.41)

\displaystyle H_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\sigma_{2}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+O(\partial_{\xi}),

(E.42)

\displaystyle V_{\not{\partial}}{}(0)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial_{1}m(0)\partial_{x_{2}}-\partial_{2}m(0)\partial_{x_{1}}}{\sqrt{\big\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\big\|_{g}^{2}+B(0)^{2}}}+O(\partial_{\xi})=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}m(0)^{\perp_{g}}}{\sqrt{\big\|\nabla_{g}m(0)\big\|_{g}^{2}+B(0)^{2}}}+O(\partial_{\xi}).

Varying the origin of the normal coordinate system, we obtain a formula analogous to (
E.21
):

(E.43)

d\pi(V_{\not{\partial}}{})(x)=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}m(x)^{\perp_{g}}}{\sqrt{\big\|\nabla_{g}m(x)\big\|_{g}^{2}+B(x)^{2}}},\qquad x\in m^{-1}(0).

E.4.
Anisotropic Haldane models

In this section, we study symbols of the form

(E.44)

{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)=\left[\begin{matrix}m(x)&\overline{\alpha(x)}\cdot\big(\xi-\xi(x)\big)\\
\alpha(x)\cdot\big(\xi-\xi(x)\big)&-m(x)\end{matrix}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\alpha_{j}(x)\cdot(\xi-\xi(x))\sigma_{j}+m(x)\sigma_{3}

where
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+i\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
;
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
; and
m\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
.

We first motivate the origin of (
E.44
) as an effective Hamiltonians for distorsions of standard models of condensed matter physics. We then pull out a metric and a magnetic field such that (
E.44
) equals a symbol of the form (
E.36
). By analogy with §
E.3
, this allows us to produce a formula for the speed of propagating modes. At the physical level, our result implies that adiabatic variation in the position of atoms slows down electronic transport along interfaces between topological insulators.

E.4.1.
Tight-binding models

Our starting point is Wallace’s Hamiltonian:

(E.45)

(H_{0}\psi)_{n}=\left[\begin{matrix}\psi_{n}^{B}+\psi_{n-v_{1}}^{B}+\psi_{n-v_{2}}^{B}\\
\psi_{n}^{A}+\psi_{n+v_{1}}^{A}+\psi_{n+v_{2}}^{A}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad v_{1}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad v_{2}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
-\sqrt{3}\end{matrix}\right],

with wavefunction
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}v_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}v_{2},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. This Hamiltonian describes the motion of an electron on a honeycomb lattice, with hopping probabilities equally weighted on the nearest neighbors; see Figure
E.2
. Given the periodicity of
H
with respect to the lattice
\mathbb{Z}v_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}v_{2}
, it is natural to take its discrete Fourier transform:

(E.46)

H_{0}(\xi)=\left[\begin{matrix}0&{\omega}(\xi)\\
\overline{{\omega}(\xi)}&0\end{matrix}\right],\qquad{\omega}(\xi)=1+e^{i\xi v_{1}}+e^{i\xi v_{2}}.

The eigenvalues of
H(\xi)
are
\pm\big|{\omega}(\xi)\big|
. In particular, they are degenerate precisely when
\xi=\pm\xi_{\star}\mod 2\pi
, where
\xi_{\star}=(2\pi/3,-2\pi/3)
. The crossing is conical:

(E.47)

{\omega}(\xi_{\star}+\xi)=\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{4}(\xi_{1}+i\xi_{2})+O(\xi^{2}).

In particular, the model (
E.45
) describes a semimetal.

Small perturbations of semimetals generally transforms them in insulators. In a seminal work
[
H88
]
, Haldane introduced a time-breaking Hermitian perturbation of Wallace’s model that was the first model of topological insulator beyond the quantum Hall effect. Specifically:

(E.48)

\begin{gathered}(H_{m}\psi)_{n}=(H_{0}\psi)_{n}+im\left[\begin{matrix}\psi_{n+v_{1}}^{A}-\psi_{n-v_{1}}^{A}-\psi_{n+v_{2}}^{A}+\psi_{n-v_{2}}^{A}+\psi_{n+v_{2}-v_{1}}^{A}-\psi_{n+v_{1}-v_{2}}^{A}\\
\psi_{n+v_{1}}^{B}-\psi_{n-v_{1}}^{B}-\psi_{n+v_{2}}^{B}+\psi_{n-v_{2}}^{B}-\psi_{n+v_{1}-v_{2}}^{B}+\psi_{n-v_{1}+v_{2}}^{B}\end{matrix}\right],\\
H_{m}(\xi)=H_{0}(\xi)+2im\beta(\xi)\sigma_{3},\qquad\beta(\xi)=\sin(\xi v_{1})-\sin(\xi v_{2})-\sin\big(\xi(v_{1}-v_{2})\big).\end{gathered}

In brief words, the states associated to the valence band (i.e. the negative energy eigenstates of
H_{m}(\xi)
, seen as functions on a
2
-torus), form a vector bundle with non-trivial topology: its Chern number is
{\operatorname{sgn}}(m)
. We also comment that
\beta(\xi_{\star})=3\sqrt{3}/2
.

Figure E.2.
A honeycomb structure. An electron on a red site can hop to the nearest blue site (and conversely).

The standard approach to study systems such as (
E.48
) near momentum
\xi_{\star}
is to look for solutions of
(D_{t}+H_{m})\psi=0
that take the form
\psi_{n}(t)=\Psi(ht,hn)e^{i\xi_{\star}n}
. This produces the effective equation

(E.49)

\left(hD_{t}+\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\left[\begin{matrix}m&hD_{1}-ihD_{2}\\
hD_{1}+ihD_{2}&-m\end{matrix}\right]\right)\Psi=0.

By slowly changing the sign of
m
over space, we can generate structures whose topology is globally ill-defined but locally well defined when
m\neq 0
. The set
m^{-1}(0)
acts as an interface between insulating phases of distinct Chern numbers. The resulting effective equation is:

(E.50)

\left(hD_{t}+\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\left[\begin{matrix}m(x)&hD_{1}-ihD_{2}\\
hD_{1}+ihD_{2}&-m(x)\end{matrix}\right]\right)\Psi=0,

which is precisely that studied in §
E.1
. According to Theorem
A.2
, solutions to (
E.50
) split in two parts, one transporting in a direction prescribed by topology (specifically, the positive-topology region lies to the right of the direction of propagation); and one dispersing.

While defects do not affect the insulating character of topological phases, they can influence the speed or profile of conduction along interfaces. To investigate these effects, we introduce anisotropy in the system. This corresponds to a Wallace model with the function

(E.51)

{\omega}_{a}(\xi)=1-a_{1}-a_{2}+a_{1}e^{i\xi_{1}}+a_{2}e^{i\xi_{2}}

instead of
{\omega}(\xi)
in (
E.46
); the parameter
a=(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
acounts for anistropic hopping probabilities
\frac{1-a_{1}-a+2}{3}
,
\frac{1+a_{1}}{3}
,
\frac{1+a_{2}}{3}
, see Figure
E.4
. For sufficiently small
a
, the equation
{\omega}_{a}(\xi)=0
still has precisely two solutions
\pm(\xi_{\star}+\xi_{a})
modulo
2\pi
; this means that such perturbations preserve the semimetal character. Since we have

(E.52)

{\omega}_{a}(\xi_{\star}+\xi)=\partial_{\xi}{\omega}_{a}(\xi_{a})\cdot(\xi-\xi_{a})+O(\xi-\xi_{a})^{2},

an effective operator for the anisotropic Haldane model near momentum
\xi_{\star}-\xi_{a}
is

(E.53)

\left[\begin{matrix}0&{\overline{\partial_{\xi}{\omega}_{a}(\xi_{a})}}\cdot(D_{x}-\xi_{a})\\
{\omega}_{a}(\xi_{a})\cdot(D_{x}-\xi_{a})&0\end{matrix}\right].

Figure E.4.
A distorted honeycomb structure. We drew the original hexagonal structure in the background (light gray).

Adding complex coupling as in
[
H88
]
, and allowing the hopping probabilities and complex coupling to depend on
x
, we end up with an effective Hamiltonian
\not{D}={\not{\partial}}{}^{w}
with symbol

(E.54)

{\not{\partial}}{}(x,\xi)=\left[\begin{matrix}m(x)&\alpha(x)\big(\xi-\xi(x)\big)\\
\alpha(x)\big(\xi-\xi(x)\big)&-m(x)\end{matrix}\right].

This Hamiltonian models a honeycomb lattice with microscopic unit cell, submitted to a macroscopic strain.

E.4.2.
Pseudo metric and magnetic field

Write

(E.55)

\sigma(x)\xi=(\alpha_{j}(x)\cdot\xi)\sigma_{j}.

In analogy with §
E.3
, we define a metric
g
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by (its action on the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}
):

(E.56)

|\xi|^{2}_{g(x)}=\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\alpha_{j}(x)\cdot\xi)^{2}=(\sigma(x)\xi)^{2}.

Following (
E.24
), we set

(E.57)

\sigma_{3}(x)=\dfrac{1}{i}\sigma(x)\xi\cdot\sigma(x)\xi^{\perp_{g}},\qquad|\xi|_{g(x)}=1,

which is independent of (adequately normalized)
\xi
. By plugging for
\xi
a vector in a basis dual to
\alpha_{1}(x),\alpha_{2}(x)
, we end up with

(E.58)

\sigma_{3}(x)=\det\big[\alpha_{1}(x),\alpha_{2}(x)\big]\sigma_{3}.

Hence, the symbol (
E.54
) has the form (
E.36
) for a domain wall
{\widetilde{m}}=m/\det[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]
and a magnetic potential
A(x)=\xi(x)
. From
dA=Bd\operatorname{Vol}_{g}
, we deduce that the effective magnetic field is

(E.59)

B(x)=\dfrac{\partial_{2}\xi_{1}(x)-\partial_{1}\xi_{2}(x)}{\det[\alpha_{1}(x),\alpha_{2}(x)]}.

This implies that to a distorted Haldane model correspond an emerging metric and magnetic field. This relates to the numerical and experimental observation of pseudomagnetism in strained graphene
[
MJ+13
,
GRW21
]
. Based on this analogy and on (
E.43
), our microlocal framework produces a direct formula for the (physical) speed of the propagating state:

(E.60)

\begin{gathered}d\pi(V_{\not{\partial}}{})(x)=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}{\widetilde{m}}(x)^{\perp_{g}}}{\sqrt{\big\|\nabla_{g}{\widetilde{m}}(x)\big\|_{g}^{2}+B(x)^{2}}}=\dfrac{\nabla_{g}m(x)^{\perp_{g}}}{\sqrt{\big\|\nabla_{g}m(x)\big\|_{g}^{2}+\|d\xi(x)\|_{g}^{2}}},\qquad x\in m^{-1}(0),\end{gathered}

where we used the definition of
B
; and the relation between
m
and
{\widetilde{m}}
together with
x\in m^{-1}(0)
. This implies that when appropriately measured, microscopic variations in atomic position can only slow down the propagation of edge states.
